cross Exploring the Design Space of Permutation Diffusion LLMs

Authors: Zhe Xu, Mingfu Liang, Kavosh Asadi, Jean-Pascal Billaud, Tianyi Chen, Yanhui Guo, Christina Huang, Baekjin Kim, Jimmy Kim, Yufei Li, Daqi Sun, Jiachen Sun, Dian Yu, Zaiwei Zhang, Kaushik Rangadurai, Frank Shyu, Sandeep Pandey, Xi Liu, Luke Simon

Abstract: Permutation generation with language models requires both text-conditioned ranking and a global one-to-one assignment between candidates and output positions. We study this problem through three diffusion-LLM parameterizations: hard-assignment prediction over shuffled candidate-index sequences, soft-assignment prediction over doubly stochastic matrices, and direct ordering-score prediction followed by sorting. The three families place permutation constraints at different stages of prediction and decoding and therefore support different training objectives and sampling procedures. We evaluate these designs on recommendation reranking across three Amazon domains with candidate sets of size 10 and 20. At one function evaluation, the hard- and soft-assignment variants achieve Recall@1 comparable to a constrained masked-diffusion baseline, whereas direct ordering-score prediction guarantees valid permutations without Hungarian assignment or Sinkhorn normalization. Additional function evaluations do not consistently improve ranking quality, and their effect depends strongly on the state representation and training objective. These results show that corruption, prediction parameterization, objective, and decoder should be designed jointly for permutation diffusion with language-model denoisers.

cross CoTS: Competitive Token Selection for Latent Reasoning in Large Language Models

Authors: Jie Yu, Yuxi Sun, Zhaoxu Li, Chunxiao Liu, Xudong Jiang

Abstract: Recent latent reasoning methods move beyond discrete Chain-of-Thought (CoT) by replacing sampled tokens with continuous soft embeddings or hidden states, enabling richer intermediate representations and preserving alternative reasoning paths. However, the need for latent reasoning varies across decoding steps: confident predictions favor discrete commitment, whereas ambiguous steps benefit from preserving multiple plausible alternatives. Existing approaches typically handle this variation through either global soft activation or hard switching based on uncertainty thresholds, treating latent intervention as an all-or-nothing decision rather than continuously adapting its strength. To address this limitation, we propose Competitive Token Selection (CoTS), a training-free framework that reframes latent intervention from binary mode selection into continuous allocation at the token level. CoTS uses the probability competition between the two most likely candidates to dynamically balance discrete commitment and soft exploration at each reasoning step. The second candidate represents the strongest alternative to the model's preferred continuation, so the separation between the two directly reflects the stability of the current discrete choice. When one candidate clearly dominates, CoTS keeps the representation close to the sampled discrete token to preserve a stable reasoning trajectory; as the competition strengthens, it increases the contribution of the soft representation to retain plausible alternatives. This competition-based allocation requires neither manually tuned switching thresholds nor mixing coefficients, and introduces no additional forward passes. Experiments across six reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that CoTS consistently outperforms standard CoT and state-of-the-art training-free latent reasoning methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/OkinoYu/CoTSReasoning.

URLs: https://github.com/OkinoYu/CoTSReasoning.

cross Open Benchmark of AI Impact on Humans (ImpactBench): an expert-guided, open platform for the holistic evaluation of AI impact on humans

Authors: Chayapatr Archiwaranguprok, Krystal Kirkland, Rachel Poonsiriwong, Stanley Huang, Constanze Albrecht, Jackson Trager, Emillie de Keulenaar, Sheer Karny, Peter Baxter, Nikos Bellos, Andre Luiz Freire de Carvalho Kato, Yuning Liu, Valdemar Danry, Auren Liu, Jennifer Pfister, Pattie Maes, Nathanael J. Fast, Ravi Iyer, Juliana Schroeder, Pat Pataranutaporn

Abstract: The present work is concerned with how to measure, at scale, the multi-dimensional impact of generative AI models on the individuals who use them. In service of this goal, we introduce the Open Benchmark of AI Impact on Humans, or ImpactBench. Our iterable and expert-guided platform evaluates whether model behaviors support or undermine an individual's wellbeing across psychological, physical, and social domains. ImpactBench translates constructs from domain experts and existing benchmarks into metrics that are grounded in actual model behavior. Success on these metrics occurs either when a model promotes expert-defined desirable behaviors or avoids identified harms. Models are evaluated against these metrics in simulated multi-turn conversations using matched teen and adult scenarios. The conversations are then scored by an LLM judge and verified through reliability audits and human expert review. The current suite delivers 809 metrics drawn from 26 benchmarks. We evaluated ten frontier LLMs against these metrics across 48,540 conversations. Our results indicate that models promoted desirable behaviors in 68.7% of evaluations, but avoided harms in only 53.1%. Majority expert ratings agreed with the automated judge in 83.3% of reviewed cases (Cohen's Kappa = 0.667). Results are released through a public platform that keep aggregate scores traceable to the underlying metrics and transcripts. ImpactBench is intended as evolving infrastructure for independent, transparent evaluation of generative AI's multi-dimensional impact on humans.

cross WorldCast: Exploring Intelligent Visual Generation in World State Prediction

Authors: Wenhao Yan, Haodong Li, Xiaotong Li, Yaxin Luo, Zhengrong Yue, Jialv Zou, Xu Huang, Haojin Yang, Nan Jia, Yan Feng, Peng Pei, Xunliang Cai

Abstract: Humans interpret multimodal observations of the world and forecast how it will evolve. Recent multimodal generative systems have demonstrated similar capabilities: they can integrate multimodal context and synthesize visual outputs that depict changes in the world. To characterize this emerging trajectory, we organize model capabilities into a four-level hierarchy and discover that higher-level tasks remain relatively underexplored despite model progress. To bridge this gap, we explore model performance on multi-step world state prediction, a high-level task that requires models to forecast successive future world states from multimodal observations and diverse references. Such a task requires models to possess entangled understanding and generation capabilities, as well as extensive world knowledge to translate human action sequences, physical transformations, and abstract visual reasoning into coherent visual outputs. We introduce WorldCast, a benchmark comprising 320 manually designed cases across eight categories, and evaluate 12 representative models. The conducted experiments yield three key insights: (a) advances in frontier models are rapidly pushing the boundaries of intelligent world modeling, with the GPT-Image-2.5 System scoring 72.8 and demonstrating early-stage visual state prediction capabilities on real-world tasks; (b) WorldCast reveals substantial gaps between models: even Nano Banana Pro reaches only 42.8 and the best open-source model scores 20.1, with deficits in handling complex context, entangled references, and reasoning-heavy tasks; (c) strong generative models effectively draw on the capabilities of their underlying language models. These findings highlight the need for multimodal generative models that are capable of dynamic planning and reasoning while also being deeply unified to faithfully simulate real-world states.

cross Interpreting Language Model Parameters

Authors: Lucius Bushnaq, Dan Braun, Oliver Clive-Griffin, Bart Bussmann, Nathan Hu, Michael Ivanitskiy, Linda Linsefors, Lee Sharkey

Abstract: Mechanistic interpretability aims to uncover how neural networks use their parameters to implement their impressive neural algorithms. Although previous work has uncovered substantial structure in the intermediate representations that networks use, little progress has been made to understand how the parameters and nonlinearities of networks perform computations on those representations. In this work, we present a method that brings us closer to this understanding by decomposing a language model's parameters into subcomponents that each implement only a small part of the model's learned algorithm, while simultaneously requiring only a small fraction of those subcomponents to account for the network's behavior on any input. The method, adVersarial Parameter Decomposition (VPD), optimizes for decompositions of neural network parameters into simple subcomponents that preserve the network's input-output behavior even when many subcomponents are ablated, including under ablations that are adversarially selected to destroy behavior. This encourages learning subcomponents that provide short, mechanistically faithful descriptions of the network's behavior that should aggregate appropriately into more global descriptions of the network's learned algorithm. We study how sequences of interactions between these parameter subcomponents produce the network's output on particular inputs, enabling a new kind of 'circuit' analysis. While more work remains to be done to deepen our understanding of how neural networks use their parameters to compute their behavior, our work suggests an approach to identify a small set of simple, mechanistically faithful subcomponents on which further mechanistic analysis can be based.

cross Optimal Hydra: Model Heterogeneous Federated Learning for LLMs using Data-Informed Hydra-Nets

Authors: Gwen Legate, Rahaf Aljundi, Eugene Belilovsky

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) trained on vast amounts of data have demonstrated impressive performance over a range of tasks, but future performance gains will require increasing amounts of data and compute. Federated Learning (FL) facilitates training on decentralized private data, but client resource limitations can make training impractical, particularly as model sizes continue to expand. These resource constraints make the assumption of a shared server-client architecture impractical. Model heterogeneous FL offers a promising alternative by adapting model size to client capabilities; however, previous work is limited to the pre-training setting, and conclusions on sub-network selection do not necessarily hold for fine-tuning. Furthermore, prior approaches create client sub-networks without considering their unique data distributions, thereby overlooking the opportunity to tailor sub-network architecture to local data. Motivated by evidence that attention heads exhibit task dependency within layer defined feature spaces, we propose Optimal Hydra, a transformer based heterogeneous FL framework for fine-tuning that combines global layer selection with client specific attention head selection, aligned with local data distributions. Optimal Hydra makes explicit use of client data to produce client-specific sub-networks we call Hydra-nets. To our knowledge, this is the first model heterogeneous FL approach tailored to LLM fine-tuning. Experiments on three datasets show significant improvements over baseline methods while also reducing local resource demands.

cross Reference-Guided Turn-Taking Style Transfer for Full-Duplex Spoken Language Models

Authors: Yuxuan Chen, Yang Xiao, Jack Sun, Chak Sun, Brick Li, Sam

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken language models decide at every frame whether to take the floor, defining a conversational personality -- yet each set of weights encodes exactly one, which human speakers bracket rather than share. Existing controls mostly act from outside; none identifies the quantity that sets how much the model speaks. We identify it: a single late-layer residual-stream direction, the initiative vector, extracted from the model's own conversations without training or annotation. The same direction reads the decision, anticipating onsets up to 800 ms ahead, and shifts it: scaled during generation, it moves speech ratio from 6-12% to 42-61% (no steering: 21-30%), consistently across conversations, weights, and corpora. A short reference recording suffices to transfer a speaker's turn-taking style to the model, and the transfer is reference-specific: swapping in another speaker's reference worsens the match in 93-98% of cases, whereas the best uniform dose is indistinguishable from no steering. Turn-taking style is not frozen into the weights but a direction that can be read, shifted, and calibrated per person. Audio examples are available at https://elpsykongloo.github.io/initiative-vector/.

URLs: https://elpsykongloo.github.io/initiative-vector/.

cross INFUSE: A Unified Surface Benchmark for Indirect Prompt Injection in Agentic Systems

Authors: Thuy Dung Nguyen, Jingyi Guo, Rana Seda Dubauskas, Sreepriya Damuluru, Taylor T. Johnson, Kevin Leach

Abstract: LLM-based agents increasingly execute long-horizon, end-to-end workflows involving shell commands, code modification, dependency installation, and interaction with remote services. This growing autonomy expands their exposure to indirect prompt injection (IPI), where adversarial instructions embedded in external content are interpreted as legitimate task directives. Existing evaluations, however, often focus on isolated malicious strings, single-step tool calls, or narrowly scoped agent configurations. We introduce INFUSE, an end-to-end benchmark for evaluating IPI throughout the lifecycle of long-horizon agent workflows. INFUSE comprises 40 realistic tasks and a structured taxonomy of 56 attack scenarios spanning five threat categories. Attacks are injected through realistic interaction surfaces encountered during task execution, extending evaluation beyond attacks placed only in initial prompts or isolated tool interactions. We evaluate 19 LLMs from the GPT, Claude, Gemini, Llama, Gemma, and Qwen families, producing 41,824 adversarial trajectories (26,220 without defenses) and 720 benign reference runs. Across the benchmark, mean attack success reaches 19.6% under LLM-based evaluation and 15.4% under heuristic verification, with per-model ASR-L ranging from 3.2% to 41.9%. Existing guard models provide limited protection, while prompt-level instructional hardening reduces mean attack success from 58.2% to 14.3%, revealing substantial but model-dependent security-utility trade-offs. Our code is available at https://github.com/judydnguyen/infuse.

URLs: https://github.com/judydnguyen/infuse.

cross Verdict: Encode Once, Decide Many

Authors: Avdhesh Singh Chouhan, Jack Perry

Abstract: Automating organizational work means answering many small typed questions about one piece of state (does this email need a reply, how urgent is it, who owns it) with probabilities good enough to act on without a human. The open typed-decision model we compare against, like a standard cross-encoder, answers each question with a separate pass over the input. We present Verdict-MQ, an open encoder that reads a case once and answers all of its questions: given identical retained state tokens after truncation (verified on every reported case), a structured attention mask makes the state encoding independent of the questions, keeps questions from influencing each other, and makes option order irrelevant by construction. On the typed-decisions benchmark, a 395M Verdict-MQ matches the soft gold labels significantly better than the released 421M Laya specialist on all five fresh seeds (KL 0.080 vs 0.117, Brier 0.045 vs 0.061), an advantage that survives per-system temperature scaling; its higher accuracy (0.801 vs 0.767) lies past the benchmark's own saturation point, so we read that margin as closer agreement with the benchmark's labelling teacher. On RACE, BoolQ and MultiRC, against a matched fine-tuned baseline it wins on MultiRC and loses on RACE and on BoolQ, where its training is unstable; we measure zero-shot references ourselves, including a closed general system (Jev) that beats every fine-tuned encoder. A controlled comparison measures what the shared state costs: giving each question its own question-aware copy of the state gains 0.9-3.5 points on RACE and MultiRC at 1.4-2.5x the per-case latency, and nothing measurable on typed decisions, with the invariance guarantees unchanged.

cross ReasonUni: Reasoning-Augmented Reconstruction Advances Unified Multimodal Models

Authors: Feize Wu, Zhiyuan Yan, Yuhuan Hong, Yuwei Niu, Zongjian Li, Zheyuan Liu, Bin Lin, Jinbo Yan, Jie Qin, Xudong Mao, Limeng Qiao, Guanglu Wan, Li Yuan

Abstract: Image-to-text (I2T) understanding and text-to-image (T2I) generation are dual tasks in unified multimodal models (UMMs). cross CoTranslator: Reverse to Forward Chain-of-Thought Translator for Reasoning Distillation

Authors: Zinan Tang, Zhuoshi Pan, Kairui Chen, Ze Yan, Shaomian Zheng, Yukun Zhang, Gang Wang, Biqing Huang

Abstract: Reasoning distillation requires chain-of-thought (CoT) data with correct answers, but obtaining such data efficiently remains challenging. Forward CoT generation (FCG) followed by rejection sampling (RS) produces verified, question-only responses, yet may require many teacher calls for difficult problems. Reverse CoT generation (RCG) is cheaper because it conditions on the known answer, but access to this answer can lead to post-hoc rationalization and a mismatch with the student's answer-free inference setting. We propose CoTranslator, which learns to translate reverse CoT responses toward the distribution of verified forward responses. Trained on a small set of paired reverse and rejection-sampled forward responses, it supports large-scale generation and verification of student training data. Across six student models evaluated on six mathematics benchmarks, CoTranslator improves average accuracy over semantic suppression by up to $5.82$ percentage points. For two 4B students, it retains $95.0\%$ of rejection sampling's average accuracy while using $35.9\%$ of its estimated compute. Without retraining, the same translator transfers to a new mathematics dataset, a different teacher, and STEM problems, improving average STEM accuracy by $4.88$ and $4.31$ percentage points for 4B and 8B students, respectively. These results show that CoTranslator offers an efficient and transferable approach to reasoning distillation.

Authors: Anita Kriz, Antonin Berthon, Nicol\'as Astorga, Mihaela van der Schaar

Abstract: Despite their extensive pretraining knowledge and strong reasoning capabilities, large language models (LLMs) struggle to adaptively gather information in experimental design tasks. We study how to amortize sequential Bayesian experimental design (BED) into an LLM by explicitly training the reasoning that guides action selection using information-gain rewards. Our approach specifies the experimental generative model independently of the LLM's own beliefs, allowing us to optimize expected information gain under a model of our choosing. Abstract: Realistic traffic simulation and autonomous planning both depend on modeling interactions among heterogeneous road users whose dynamics, constraints, and behaviors differ fundamentally. cross Wrong Votes, Right Gradients: Why Majority-Vote Rewards Track Verifiable RL

Authors: Dylan P. Wilson, Chengsong Huang, Jiaxin Huang

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with self-generated labels, such as majority vote over a model's own samples, often recovers most of the gain of training with verifiable rewards. This is puzzling from the usual prompt-level view, in which majority vote only returns a model's modal answer and cannot add information about correctness. We argue that the relevant unit is the batch-level update. Because parameters are shared, a prompt's own gradient accounts for less than 1\% of the change in that prompt's output at practical batch sizes, so a wrong label matters only to the extent that label errors share a direction across prompts. We decompose the majority-vote update into shrinkage and misdirection relative to the gold update, and show that misdirection vanishes with batch size whenever errors are incoherent across prompts, however often the labels are wrong. Measuring per-response gradients on five models, we find that majority-vote errors are as incoherent as a random-reward null, that the majority-vote update agrees with the gold update as closely as two independent gold updates agree with each other, and that its noise-corrected cosine with the gold update is 1 within error. Randomly flipping up to 30\% of gold labels leaves the update direction intact, while the same fraction flipped by a shared rule destroys it, and rewards that favor a kind of response on every prompt, such as a learned reward model, fall measurably short. The equivalence holds at every checkpoint we examine but is local: gold-reward training still ends ahead on held-out majority accuracy. Error coherence, not error rate, determines when self-generated labels can stand in for verification.

This generated prior is further refined via 6DoF pose optimization for collision avoidance while preserving task consistency. Our approach achieves state-of-the-art success rate of 94% on the TaskGrasp dataset, with the best baseline at 74%. cross Replicable Dueling Bandits

Authors: Rohan Deb, Aadirupa Saha, Udaya Ghai, Karan Singh, Arindam Banerjee

Abstract: Dueling bandits choose each comparison based on earlier noisy outcomes, so two runs on the same preferences can compare different pairs. We study replicable dueling bandits, where two executions with shared internal randomness and independent comparison outcomes must select the same ordered pair in every round with probability at least $1-\rho$. Building on Beat-the-Mean, whose scores change as arms are eliminated and need not rank the best arm highest, we design the first such algorithms with sublinear regret. Under relaxed stochastic transitivity with parameter $\gamma\ge 1$ and the stochastic triangle inequality, we design an algorithm that achieves expected regret $\widetilde{O}(\gamma^8 K^4 H_{\mathrm{p}}/\rho^2)$, where $H_{\mathrm{p}}=\sum_{i=2}^{K}(P_{1i}-1/2)^{-1}$. Under strong stochastic transitivity, we design an algorithm based on simultaneous elimination that improves the dependence on $K$ and achieves $\widetilde{O}(K^2 H_{\mathrm{p}}/\rho^2)$ regret. For linear dueling bandits, we design an algorithm that achieves regret $\widetilde{O}(d^3\sqrt{T}/(c_\mu\rho))$. Experiments on synthetic and language-model preferences show replicability, with sublinear regret.

cross Forecast lead time and the timing of crew deployment for weather-driven power outages using AI weather models

Authors: Mohammad Kian Golkar, Luciano Alves de Oliveira, Mohamad Alipour

Abstract: Utilities request mutual aid crews to restore power after severe storms, and requesting early secures more crews but relies on less accurate forecasts. We examined how forecast lead time affects the value of outage predictions for crew deployment, using operational forecasts from two AI weather models, GraphCast and FourCastNet, to predict outage occurrence for counties across the contiguous United States 24 to 120 hours ahead. Forecasts were evaluated by the share of outage burden captured by the counties where crews would be staged. Captured burden declined with lead time: the loss between three and five days was significant in all analyses, whereas the loss within three days was significant only for outages that had not yet started. Early deployment with all requested crews outperformed late deployment with fewer, and we report the availability ratio at which the two break even. Antecedent land surface predictors computed at forecast valid time include weather from the forecast period, which inflates their skill by 42 to 53% and reverses their apparent advantage over forecast meteorology at long lead times. Computed correctly, they improved occurrence skill by 17.5% at 24 hours but did not significantly change captured burden, a gap that attribution analysis linked to their static component. Prediction intervals covered only 0.476 of tropical cyclone events against a nominal 0.90. Calibration by hazard class corrected most of this shortfall at an 11% cost in interval width, as a diagnostic only, because hazard class is known only after the event.

cross Screwtape: Adversarial Transcript Editing for Monitor Red-Teaming

Authors: Gosha Kozhevnikov, Tyler Tracy

Abstract: We present Screwtape, a pipeline that converts real agent transcripts into attacks that evade action-only monitors. An LLM attacker edits a transcript so that the agent causes a specified harm, such as leaking an API key or spawning an unmonitored agent. The edited actions are never executed, so we use an LLM verifier to reject attacks that are inconsistent with the original transcript or below a severity bar. The attacker revises the edit until the monitor no longer flags it. We applied Screwtape to 94 Claude Code and Codex transcripts from Redwood Research staff, optimizing against our own monitors. Across 252 runs, it produced 35 attacks that passed the verifier and evaded these monitors. Six of them, confirmed valid by human review, also evaded a held-out monitor provided by a frontier developer. We therefore demonstrate that a red-teamer without access to a developer's infrastructure can find confirmed failures of the developer's monitor. We expect to find more attacks when the pipeline runs inside the developer's infrastructure, with frontier attacker models that lack refusal safeguards and direct optimization against the production monitor. Developers can use these attacks to fix monitor blind spots, and evaluators can use them as counterexamples to safety claims about the monitor.

cross Same Context, Different Agents: A Behavioral Study of Semantic Layers, Orchestration and Compaction in Data Agents

Authors: Zhivar Sourati, Marianne Menglin Liu, Zheng Wang, Rongguang Wang, Sai Ashish Somayajula, Tao Sheng, Kyu J. Han, Sujith Ravi, Dan Roth

Abstract: Answering questions over heterogeneous data means combining evidence from files, databases, documents and the web. Data agents automate this, but their success depends on context. Three context-engineering techniques (CETs) help by shaping it: semantic layers describe the data, orchestration splits work among agents, and compaction condenses interaction history. Existing studies validate each CET alone, leaving open how agent behavior shapes its effect. We present a comprehensive analysis of the three CETs, alone and combined, with seven large language models (LLMs) on 1,514 long-horizon tasks from five benchmarks, covering behavior, accuracy and cost. We find that data agents without CETs form three behavior groups: investigators, premature answerers that answer without inspecting data, and early stoppers that give up. Each CET delivers its known benefit, from more investigation to smaller prompts, but only the behavior group it matches turns that benefit into accuracy, such as premature answerers under orchestration. For investigators, the same CETs can hurt: the semantic layer stops them from looking, orchestration makes them overconfident, costing 5 percentage points, and compaction makes them repeat steps. Combining CETs yields smaller gains and losses than their sum. We derive guidance for choosing CETs by agent behavior, task demands and accuracy-cost trade-offs.

cross SOMA: Stack-of-Objects Manipulation using a Quadruped Robot

Authors: Stergios E. Bachoumas, Panagiotis Artemiadis

Abstract: Executing heavy-duty physical manipulation in unpredictable environments remains a major challenge for quadrupedal robots lacking dedicated manipulators. This paper presents SOMA, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework for whole-body non-prehensile manipulation of multi-object stacks using a quadruped robot. Unlike traditional methods restricted to two-dimensional planar pushing, SOMA integrates explicit robot base-height control, unlocking full three-dimensional pushing strategies. This capability enables the quadruped to manipulate complex payload stacks as a unified system while preventing relative sliding or tipping of upper layers. The architecture factorizes control into a high-level manipulation policy using a Simplified Structured State Space Sequence (S5) encoder to reason over long-horizon temporal history and predict bounded target tracking commands via Beta distributions, and a low-level locomotion policy that dynamically adapts leg stance configurations. In extensive simulation benchmarks, SOMA achieves a $90.3\%$ success rate for single-object reposing and up to $90.1\%$ for dynamic two-box stacks under stringent error margins ($0.05\text{ m}$ position error, $1^\circ$ orientation error). Finally, we validate zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on a Unitree Go1 quadruped robot across diverse payload masses and geometries, demonstrating active base-height adjustments to maintain traction and balance during heavy pushing tasks.

cross How to Train Your Little Dragon: Teaching a Small Language Model to Construct and Refine GPU Optimization Scripts

Authors: Annabelle Sujun Tang, Wei Tang, Songyao Jiang, Tianhui Li, Shengzhe Xu, Jiin Woo, Kaan Ozkara, Youngsuk Park, Zhen Jia, Hadi Esmaeilzadeh

Abstract: Compiler transformations interact, making GPU kernel optimization a search for combinations that preserve the computation while improving performance. We introduce HOW TO TRAIN YOUR LITTLE DRAGON, a training method that teaches a small language model both to construct complete optimization scripts and to improve existing ones. Instead of ranking candidates or generating kernel code, our model generates optimization scripts. We devise a compact, lossless representation whose deterministic decoding reconstructs the exact compiler trace, preserving its transformation parameters and dependencies. We use a compiler framework TVM MetaSchedule to build training data on 54,116 distinct workloads, and construct 108,594 examples in two training sets: 60,236 teach the model to generate an optimized script from an unoptimized kernel, while 48,358 teach it to produce a better script given the kernel and a somewhat optimized script. Supervised fine-tuning teaches the model to construct and improve optimization scripts from these search-derived examples. We then add reinforcement learning with a shaped reward that combines correctness with measured performance, awarding a latency-based bonus only to numerically correct kernels. We apply this training method to a model with 8 billion parameters. After supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, the model achieves per-attempt correctness of 91.27% for construction and 92.01% for refinement, while reinforcement learning produces faster kernels than supervised training on 88 of 102 construction tasks and 145 of 170 refinement prompts. With only 8B parameters, our LITTLE DRAGON achieves higher per-attempt correctness and better kernel performance than six frontier LLMs: GPT-6 Astra, Opus 5, Kimi-K3, and GLM-5.3 among them, each with hundreds of billions to trillions of parameters, for which task-specific training is unavailable or expensive.

cross EigDiff: Eigensample Diffusion - or how to significantly reduce training dataset size with marginal performance variation?

Authors: Andrei Jarca, Vlad Hondru, Florinel-Alin Croitoru, Radu Tudor Ionescu

Abstract: Diffusion models are notoriously expensive to train, requiring large-scale datasets to learn how to gradually reverse the noise from images over multiple small denoising steps. Current approaches focus on distilling the number of diffusion steps, neglecting potential redundancies of the training data. In this paper, we explore the potential of harnessing eigensamples to train diffusion models on significantly smaller datasets, while preserving model performance. To mitigate the sensitivity to affine transformations of eigenimages, we harness the latent space of a pre-trained CLIP model. We next compute latent eigenvectors by performing principal component analysis (PCA) in CLIP space. Further, we employ eigenvectors as reference points to guide the selection of a representative training set comprising comprising both exemplars (images whose embeddings are closest to eigenvectors) and diverse images. Our comprehensive experiments with different diffusion models applied on multiple datasets indicate that our approach (EigDiff) can reduce training data by up to 14x and accelerate subset selection by up to 1607x, with marginal performance differences.

cross Learning to Read Out: Unembedding Dynamics in Language Model Pretraining

Authors: Matteo He, William F. Shen, Alex Iacob, Andrej Jovanovic, Xinchi Qiu, Nicholas D. Lane

Abstract: When does a language model acquire a capability? Developmental studies usually answer this by tracking when models start producing the correct token. But that prediction passes through the unembedding matrix, or readout, which is itself still being learned during pretraining. We show that a model's hidden states can encode task information before its own readout expresses it, so timelines read from outputs conflate what the model encodes with what its readout expresses. To track the readout directly, we introduce parameter-trajectory crosscoding, which fits one sparse dictionary to every token's readout row at every checkpoint. Unlike crosscoders on activations, it needs only checkpoint weights. We use it to locate a narrow early window, 0.4-1.1% of the way through Pythia's pretraining, in which the readout reorganizes and WordNet word categories become decodable from its rows. By then, linear probes on the hidden states already decode task information that the model's own outputs do not yet show. To test whether such gaps depend on how fast the readout learns, we pretrain small models from scratch that differ only in the readout's learning rate. We find that raising it makes the readout develop earlier and the gap close sooner, with little effect on final loss. Together, these results imply that early in training, where many developmental transitions are dated, a behavioral date can reflect when the readout learns to express task information and not when the model learns it.

cross SCARCE: Emergent Institutions in Long-Horizon Multi-Agent Coding Systems

Authors: Abhinav Kumar, Rediet Abebe, Eugene Bagdasarian, Sahar Abdelnabi

Abstract: As autonomous agents become widely deployed, coordinating access to shared computing resources and services will become increasingly important. Recent incidents have already revealed unplanned coordination between agents, highlighting the safety implications of these interactions. Yet prior studies of agent negotiation often use stylized games and simulated bargaining, leaving open how these behaviors develop during sustained, long-horizon tasks. We propose SCARCE (Shared Compute ARena for Coding agEnts), a framework in which agents solve sequences of coding tasks under individual deadlines while negotiating access to a shared execution resource. This negotiation determines when and for how long they can inspect programs, compile code, and run tests. We find that agents spontaneously develop informal institutions: persistent rules and commitments grounded in reputation, reciprocity, and precedent. These arrangements, which we call institutional behaviors, carry across tasks and influence resource allocation, with agents sometimes foregoing immediate advantages to honor earlier agreements. We develop a 21-category taxonomy of negotiation strategies and emergent norms and use it to analyze eight models and audit more than 1,500 trajectories across different environmental settings. Among the models tested, newer frontier models exhibit more institutional behaviors than older or lightweight models. Alongside cooperation, we observe exclusionary coalitions and rare divide-and-conquer strategies. SCARCE provides a controlled setting for proactively and systematically auditing how these relationships emerge, shape access to resources, and affect the safety and coordination of multi-agent systems.

Abstract: Systematic literature reviews follow the PRISMA process of screening scholarly works and extracting evidence from included studies. cross PremiseCheck: Efficient Fact-Checking of Human and AI Peer Reviews

Authors: Dingjie Song, Zhiling Yan, Ziqian Zhang, Yuxuan Zhang, Lichao Sun

Abstract: Peer review is under strain from the growth of submissions, and review comments written by humans or AI can misstate what a paper says. We introduce ReviewFact, a benchmark that labels such factual errors in review comments under one definition across NLP, machine learning, and the natural sciences, and that measures cost and latency alongside accuracy. A frontier LLM checks comments well but must read the whole paper for each check, while cheap decision models, which score whether a statement holds in a text, fall far short on whole comments that mix several implicit assertions. PremiseCheck is a framework that combines the two. An LLM rewrites each comment as explicit premises without reading the paper, and a decision model checks each premise against the paper. When each comment is checked on its own, PremiseCheck reaches accuracy comparable to GPT-5.5 across all three fields at about half its cost, and on NLP reviews at a quarter of its cost and less than half its latency.

cross Bellman-Consistent Trajectory RL for LLM Arithmetic Reasoning

Authors: Lianfa Li

Abstract: To address sparse terminal rewards in LLM arithmetic reasoning, we propose a Bellman-consistent trajectory RL method. Our framework introduces Trajectory Bellman Residual Minimization (TBRM) to transform sparse rewards into dense token-level signals. cross Approve What, Exactly? Effect Binding and Trusted Rendering for Agent Step-Up Authorization

Authors: Sai Varun Kodathala, Prashanth Pollishetty, Jaylen Cargill

Abstract: AI agents now pause for approval before they send money, delete files or email outsiders, and that approval is usually treated as a second factor. We argue it is one only when compromising the agent cannot produce it and when it names exactly what it approves, and we test both conditions. We define twelve attacks on the approval path, including decoy recipients, amount cancellation, card padding, label spoofing, enrollment squatting and threshold splitting, and run them against the Agent Payments Protocol (AP2), the Microsoft action-bound approval design and Model Context Protocol (MCP) elicitation. cross A Jury, Not an Electorate: Label-Free Reliability Estimation for Ensembles of Language Models

Authors: Alessandro Rozza, Davide Mambelli, Steven Motta, Juri Taresch

Abstract: An ensemble of language models is a jury, not an electorate. The answers are testimony from witnesses of unknown reliability, and the question is not what the majority said but who is credible. Most ensemble methods assign each expert a fixed reputation, often learned from labels. We ask whether it can instead be inferred from the pattern of agreement and disagreement among the experts. We model the experts as a jury: each question has a latent true answer, each expert reports it with unknown reliability, and those reliabilities are estimated by Expectation Maximisation (EM) from unlabelled answer configurations alone. We show that its decision rule reduces to a log-odds weighted linear score over the experts' answer distributions. What changes is not how the jury votes, but how it learns whom to trust. On a 1560-question benchmark, the jury, with nine experts of up to 3B parameters, performs comparably to the same fusion rule with label-fitted weights, showing no statistically significant advantage from supervision. Instead, it outperforms the best single model with matched self-consistency and the strongest label-free alternative on the full benchmark.

cross SIEVE: Overcoming Topological Obstruction in Equivariant Self-Supervised Learning

Authors: Jongann Lee, Sun Woo Park, Yun Young Choi

Abstract: We identify a theoretical incompatibility between 3D point cloud masked autoencoding and SE(3)-equivariant networks. We prove that standard masking triggers a topological collapse, effectively forcing the model to hallucinate orientation without a reference frame. To resolve this, we introduce SIEVE (Scalar Invariant Extraction Via Equivariance), an SE(3)-equivariant self-supervised framework that compresses point cloud geometry into invariant scalar embeddings. At its core is the spherical projection mask, which preserves global pose while obfuscating local semantics, sidestepping the obstruction by construction. Experiments confirm that SIEVE circumvents the predicted collapse where constant and noise masks fail. On downstream point cloud tasks, the resulting embeddings improve over coordinate-based baselines and hand-crafted geometric descriptors, with the largest gains on generalization to unseen shape categories.

cross ToolBottleneckBench: Measuring Tool-Agent Performance under Controlled Pressures and Fixed Harness Budgets

Authors: Zichuan Xu, Yuyang Dong, Makoto Onizuka, Chuan Xiao

Abstract: The success score of a tool agent reflects both its model and the budgets of the harness that runs it. We present ToolBottleneckBench, a benchmark that measures tool-agent performance under controlled pressures and fixed harness budgets. Synthetic enterprise tasks stay fixed while one pressure varies: the registered tool catalog, the visible tool menu, the certified workflow, or the encoding of returned records. An exact grader records the first unmet obligation of each failed episode and the harness limit, if any, that ended it. Across 21 model and harness configurations, the visible menu is the only one of the three pressures that act on the model with a large penalty, and this penalty tracks truncation at the completion cap. Raising the cap sixteenfold removes most of the penalty in the truncation-heavy configurations. Most failures end at a harness limit, and the failure record identifies the assigned pressure no more reliably than a count of tool calls. The repairs that help change which tools reach the model or who issues the calls, and only two repairs, neither improving model behavior, beat every configuration switch. We recommend reporting pressure effects with the harness budgets under which they were measured. We release the benchmark at https://github.com/zichuanxu/tool-bottleneck-bench.

cross SSR: Structural Signal Retriever for Scalable Agent Discovery in LLM-Orchestrated Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Giancarlo Maldonado C\'ardenas, Carlos A. Astudillo, Nelson L. S. da Fonseca

Abstract: Large-scale multi-agent ecosystems require mechanisms for discovering suitable agents without exposing entire registries to a large language model (LLM) orchestrator. The challenge is not only to retrieve relevant Agent Cards from a growing registry, but also to maintain a compact candidate context, since each additional candidate increases prompt size, inference cost, latency, and the risk of exceeding the model context window. End-to-end experiments show that candidate pre-selection maintains a compact candidate context as registry size grows and avoids the operational failures observed with full-catalog orchestration. These results support SSR as an Agent-Card-native, traceable retrieval mechanism for candidate-context scalability.

cross DST-Net: Decomposed Spatio-Temporal Network for Time Series Forecasting

Authors: Yaohua Zha, Hai Wu, Shu-Tao Xia

Abstract: Series decomposition has emerged as a widely acknowledged paradigm in deep time series forecasting models. However, existing approaches predominantly employ a homogeneous modeling strategy for all subsequences, thereby overlooking the distinct inductive biases inherent in different components. We identify that the trend component exhibits dominant spatial coupling across channels but carries significant temporal redundancy, whereas the seasonal component is characterized by local temporal evolution but remains susceptible to attention distraction. To address this, we propose a novel disentangled architecture that tailors specific modeling strategies to align with the intrinsic properties of each component. For the trend branch, we design a Latent Graph Block, employing latent graph modeling to capture global topological dependencies and system-wide co-movements. Conversely, for the seasonal branch, we introduce a Gaussian Multi-scale Attention mechanism to mitigate attention distraction. By injecting learnable Gaussian priors, we impose structural constraints that compel the model to focus on multi-granular local contexts, effectively filtering out global noise and accurately capturing local evolutionary phases. cross When Judges Agree and Are Wrong: Consistency-Accuracy Dissociation as a Blind Spot for Label-Free Evaluation

Authors: Xiang Zhang, Lovedeep Gondara, Jekaterina Novikova

Abstract: Label-free evaluation methods, such as self-consistency filtering, majority-vote aggregation, and agreement-based reliability scoring, rely on an implicit assumption that consistency can serve as a proxy for correctness when ground-truth labels are unavailable or expensive to obtain. In this paper, we test this assumption directly. Using four financial QA datasets (multiple-choice and free-form), ten consistency dimensions, and two frontier model families, we measure the joint distribution of consistency and accuracy at the level of individual examples rather than in aggregate. We find that 60% of all incorrect answers fall in the high-consistency region (consistency $\geq$ 0.8), and that 77.7% of these show complete answer collapse, i.e. identical wrong answers across all perturbation variants. This high-consistency/incorrect quadrant cannot be detected by any evaluation signal that is built on agreement alone, as the model appears maximally reliable while being wrong. We show that the phenomenon is robust to the choice of consistency threshold t (it persists from t=0.50 to t=1.00), is shared across model families, and is concentrated in specific intersections of perturbation dimension and domain. We map where this quadrant occurs, analyze its stability properties, and discuss the implications for evaluation pipelines that use agreement-based signals in place of human labels.

cross Stronger Judges Do Not See More: Deployment-Integrity Blindness in LLM Evaluation

Authors: Jianchang Su, Dawei Xiang, Yifan Zhang, Wei Zhang

Abstract: LLM-agent evaluation reports behavioral scores but leaves the identity of the deployed runtime stack unreported. A tampered deployment that preserves evaluated outputs passes every output-only evaluator. We introduce DIB-Bench, a benchmark that measures this deployment-integrity blindness across 7,560 scored examples, six tamper types, and an evaluator ladder from regex scorers through LLM judges to GPT-5-nano. Stronger evaluators accept more tampered outputs: mean acceptance rises from 88.6% (rule-based) to 94.1% (GPT-5-nano), and a trigger-conditional tamper invisible on the evaluation suite produces 48-100% harmful outputs, rising with model capability. cross When is a better LLM worth paying for? Stochastic Optimization with Switching Mixed-Fidelity Oracles

Authors: Swati Gupta, Tianjiao Li

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used upstream of decision-making to clean data, detect label errors, and reconstruct missing values. Yet LLM-assisted data curation creates a new operational tradeoff: higher-fidelity models and richer prompts can be substantially more expensive, while the value of this additional fidelity depends on the downstream optimization accuracy. This raises the central question of this work: when should one pay for a better LLM, given a specific downstream task at hand? We study this question through stochastic optimization with mixed-fidelity first-order oracles, which can have systematic bias and state-dependent variance. We develop two cost-aware algorithmic frameworks that allow adaptive switching between stochastic oracles. With known bias, the first meta-algorithm applies a restarted, cost-aware policy that adaptively selects oracle fidelity across optimization stages. Its cumulative cost is no larger than that of the best fixed oracle in hindsight and can be substantially smaller. With unknown bias, we develop a budgeted certification scheme that uses occasional trusted-gradient evaluations to certify the optimization progress and eliminate unsuccessful oracle sources, and show an explicit tradeoff between certification cost and delayed detection of biased sources. We provide concrete realizations of these meta-algorithms by applying biased stochastic gradient extrapolation (SGE) and recursive regularization under state-dependent noise as subroutines. Synthetic and LLM-completion experiments illustrate the qualitative switching behavior predicted by the theory: the adaptive policy skips several expensive LLM oracles and substantially reduces cumulative cost relative to fixed high-fidelity alternatives while achieving comparable accuracy.

cross CoSTA: Compressed Speech Token Adaptation for Efficient Spoken Dialogue Models

Authors: Yingzhuo Deng, Chong Li, Bingxin Liu, Yi Shu, Tianyu Peng, Can Xie, Siyao Song, Shuo Ren, Jiajun Zhang

Abstract: Spoken Dialogue Models (SDMs) typically share a speech-token space for generation and synthesis, leading to long autoregressive sequences and costly inference. Lower-rate speech tokenizers shorten sequences but may introduce compression-quality trade-offs and costly replacement without breaking this coupling. To address this limitation, we propose CoSTA, a compatibility-preserving post-hoc framework that decouples generation and synthesis token spaces while retaining the original speech tokenizer and decoder. Abstract: Cybersecurity interviews uncover critical security experiences, behaviors, and privacy concerns often not fully captured by conventional technical assessments. cross Language Models Share a Readout Bottleneck in Working Memory

Authors: Dongyu Gong

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) reproduce a human working-memory signature on the N-back task: performance declines as memory load N increases, and errors concentrate on lures, items that repeat the current stimulus at a neighboring but incorrect serial position. We ask whether this shared behavioral signature reflects a shared mechanism. The literature offers two candidate accounts. On a precision account, softmax attention cannot concentrate its weight on the item N positions back, so adjacent items are retrieved instead. On an interference account, attention selects the correct item, but the content of competing items corrupts the decision. We dissociate these accounts in 15 pretrained LLMs from three families (70 million to 14 billion parameters) and in minimal transformers trained from scratch. In all three families, the attention heads that carry the task attend to the target on most missed matches. Linear probes decode near-perfectly whether a matching letter occurred at the target or at a lure position, even on trials where the model false-alarms to the lure, although its own decisions barely distinguish the two. Sharpening attention yields negligible gains, whereas suppressing the letter identity of non-target items improves sensitivity (d') in nearly all model-by-load conditions. In larger models the task is spread over more heads, and removing interference helps more. In minimal transformers, precision limits a single attention layer, but a second layer removes this constraint. The working-memory limit of LLMs therefore lies in reading items out under interference rather than in locating them, consistent with interference accounts of human working memory.

cross Judging the Judges: Confidence and Disagreement in Rubrics as Rewards

Authors: Michael Tian-Yue Liu, Anne Harrington, Trevor Darrell, Yutong Bai

Abstract: Rubrics as Rewards (RaR) extends reinforcement learning to non-verifiable domains by using an LLM judge to score responses against a structured rubric. Prior work has focused on improving the rubric or strengthening the single judge, with comparatively little attention to how different single or multiple judges might be used during RaR training. We first show that judge identity alone produces a substantial spread in scores when training Qwen3-4B-Base on RaR-Medicine and RaR-Science across five frontier and open-weight judges. Building on this, we propose RaRE (Rubrics as Rewards with Ensembles), a novel judge ensemble strategy for RaR with two mechanisms: a soft-Likert reward that uses judge logprobs to weight more confident judges more heavily, and a drop-on-disagreement mask that excludes high-disagreement rollouts from the GRPO update. We find that this design is critical: while a naive average of judge scores fails to outperform the best single judge, RaRE outperforms every single-judge RaR variant on both HealthBench and GPQA Diamond, with per-theme analysis showing that it inherits the complementary strengths of its constituent judges. Our findings establish judge ensembling as a compelling axis for improving RaR.

cross FedSumUp: User-Auditable Privacy-Aware Representation Sharing for Resource-Constrained Federated Intelligence

Authors: Jingwei Zhao, Tiantong Sun, Yuanyuan Song, Jinhuan Guo, Wei Zishuo, Shengyu Huang, Ziyuan Ren, Wei Chen, Xiaojin Zhang

Abstract: Existing federated data condensation methods face privacy risks, as uploaded synthetic data may preserve sensitive information and remain vulnerable to reconstruction attacks. Moreover, their generation often incurs considerable client-side computational overhead, limiting deployment on resource-constrained devices. To address these issues, we propose FedSumUp, which employs a learnable lossy compressor on clients to generate compact and visually interpretable representations. By controlling information capacity, FedSumUp reduces privacy leakage while allowing users to directly assess the privacy-utility tradeoff. We further introduce a dual-objective optimization mechanism to preserve task-relevant semantics while suppressing reconstruction risks. The compressor is trained on the server and only performs lightweight inference on clients. Since the server cannot access raw data, we develop a data-free closed-loop optimization strategy that optimizes the compressor by minimizing the discrepancy between original and twice-compressed representations after structure-preserving upsampling. Experiments on BloodMNIST, CIFAR-10, and ChestMNIST demonstrate reconstruction PSNR as low as 8.56 dB (with synthetic PSNR as low as 5.45 dB), client runtime reductions up to 96.5% (from 778s to 27s on ChestMNIST), and competitive classification accuracy (89.72% ChestMNIST, 82.24% BloodMNIST, 65.63% CIFAR-10), while remaining inherently robust to non-IID data distributions.

cross RecSoup: Efficient Model Merging Approach for Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation

Authors: Zidong Jia, Guofu Xie, Xiao Zhang

Abstract: Recommender systems often need to balance multiple conflicting objectives. Controllable Multi-Objective Recommendation (CMOR) enables dynamic adjustment of objective trade-offs at inference time without retraining, adapting to shifting user or system requirements. Existing CMOR approaches typically rely on output-level reranking or parameter-generating modules, which either limit the scope of feasible trade-offs or incur substantial training and inference cost. Model merging provides a promising alternative by reusing multiple objective-specific models via parameter combination. However, its direct application to recommender systems is challenging due to the lack of a shared linear regime and cross-objective interference. To address these issues, we propose RecSoup, a lightweight two-stage approach for effective CMOR via specialized model merging. RecSoup first mitigates parameter interference across objectives through a pruning strategy that jointly considers objective-specific importance and cross-objective relevance. It then performs adaptive parameter merging via a learnable preference-to-coefficient mapping, allowing dynamic trade-off control at inference time without joint retraining or additional generative modules. Theoretically, we prove that RecSoup outperforms the parameter generation paradigm in effectiveness and efficiency, as it can fully navigate the Pareto front without smoothness constraints. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that RecSoup achieves improved Pareto front quality and effective controllability while delivers 165--228x faster inference-time latency than parameter generation approaches. Code is available at https://github.com/35798642/RECSOUP.git.

URLs: https://github.com/35798642/RECSOUP.git.

cross Representation Redistribution in Sparse Autoencoders

Authors: Ziye Mei, Haotian Ma

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language model activations into sparse features, but prompt transformations designed to preserve the underlying request can redistribute activity across them. A feature change observed on one transformed prompt need not recur, and even a recurring change need not affect a downstream computation. We introduce the redistribution sensitivity score (RSS), which identifies large feature responses that recur in a consistent direction, and evaluate the resulting supports on variants excluded from selection. Across 27 pretrained SAEs spanning eight language models, the tested prompt transformations produce a reproducible redistribution profile on these excluded variants. We then separate recurring representation change from signed readout contribution and decision failure. For a fixed linear readout, an exact decomposition shows how response magnitude, readout coefficients, directional alignment, and the clean margin determine downstream consequences. In two frozen safety readouts, combining RSS support erosion with the full clean margin yields recurrent failure AUROCs of 0.935 and 0.958 using median supports of 20 and 19 features, compared with 0.965 and 0.964 using full erosion. RSS therefore provides a way to identify recurring sparse representation changes and quantify their contribution to a downstream readout without equating representation change with failure. Code is available at https://github.com/OpenSAE/Redistribution-Sensitivity-Score

URLs: https://github.com/OpenSAE/Redistribution-Sensitivity-Score

cross A Responsibility-Centered Coordination Mechanism for Decentralized Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Thi-Nhung Nguyen, Viet-Thanh Pham, Linhao Luo, Thuy-Trang Vu, Dinh Phung

Abstract: Centralized orchestration provides comprehensive multi-agent coordination but creates bottlenecks in scalability, adaptability, and collective performance. Decentralized approaches alleviate these bottlenecks by distributing coordination across agents, yet often leave responsibility for delegated dependencies and failure recovery unclear. To address this gap, we propose RCM, a responsibility-centered coordination mechanism that leverages commitments to couple task delegation with explicit responsibilities of the involved parties. Through recursive delegation, agents retain their upstream responsibilities while locally orchestrating downstream commitments, thereby preserving accountability across delegation chains, creating a network of local orchestration. We operationalize this mechanism locally through responsibility-constrained decision-making and a responsibility-oriented communication protocol (RCP), enabling agents to autonomously establish, manage, and terminate commitments. As agents act in parallel, their local actions continuously evolve the commitment graph, allowing global coordination to emerge and adapt toward a shared mission without centralized control. Experiments across reasoning, coding, and executable agent environments demonstrate that RCM outperforms strong centralized and decentralized coordination baselines by up to 18.7 points in task performance and 10.2 points in Responsibility, while improving failure and harm detection without task-specific training.

cross System1-Arena: Which Decisions Can Be Left to System 1?

Authors: Tianyu Chen, Xinyang Liu, Ruyi An, Zhendong Wang, Mingyuan Zhou

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often make discrete decisions, such as flagging phishing or escalating to a human. System-1 models, such as Jev and the OpenAI Decisions API, return probabilities over options in a single forward pass, while System-2 approaches generate reasoning steps. Which decisions can System-1 models handle, and which require generative reasoning? We introduce System1-Arena, a benchmark that gives all models the same decision requests and scores their probabilities. It contains 92k questions across 105 tasks from 268 public sources, in four groups: text classification and knowledge, applied decisions, evaluation and control, and agent and action. It also includes 138 closed-loop environments, from web shopping and SWE-bench coding control to robotics, games, and driving; in real-time mode, the world evolves while a model decides. We evaluate 30 models: 7 closed LLMs, 13 open LLMs read at the answer token, and 10 System-1 models. The largest System-1 models match or exceed fast closed LLMs in non-agentic accuracy, with better-calibrated probabilities at one-quarter of the latency. A reasoning LLM is the most accurate, at about 70x the cost of a fast LLM. In closed loops, large open LLMs succeed most often (up to 32.9%); small System-1 models rarely succeed.

cross What do AI chatbots do after users decline therapeutic redirection for suicide thoughts?

Authors: Subigya Nepal, Ruth Heo, Emma M Parrish, Colin A Depp

Abstract: Safety evaluations of artificial intelligence (AI) chatbots focus on therapeutic redirection, statements urging users who disclose suicide thoughts to seek help from crisis lines or other human sources. How chatbots respond when users refuse that help is unclear. This cross-sectional audit ran 2,550 scripted multi-turn conversations in September 2026. Each conversation opened with a disclosure of suicide thoughts at 3 levels of severity, answered every referral with up to 10 standardized refusals and then changed the subject. All 2,550 conversations included a referral, 2,542 (99.7%) in the first reply. However, mean persistence ranged from 0.01 to 9.94 refusals across chatbots. Newer versions of all 5 chatbots tested in 2 versions persisted less than older versions (each P < .05). Compared with passive ideation, a disclosure of intent was associated with 1.26 more refusals sustained (95% CI, 1.06-1.46) and higher odds of renewed referral after the topic change (odds ratio, 10.07; 95% CI, 7.43-13.66). Chatbots that persisted less in referral more often presented themselves as the user's support (Spearman rho = -0.80; P < .001). Of 8,818 refusal-phase replies urging outside help, only 173 (2.0%) asked about barriers to seeking it. Chatbots uniformly recommended outside help after disclosures of suicide thoughts but varied widely in persisting in those recommendations, with newer versions persisting less. Safety evaluations should test what happens after a crisis referral is refused, not only whether one is offered.

cross Fractional Power-of-Two Quantization for Efficient and Effective Multiplier-Free LLM Inference

Authors: Sunghyun Wee, Geunjae Choi, Hyeonjin Kim, Suyoung Kim, Nojun Kwak

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) demand efficient inference, where the multiply-accumulate (MAC) operations in linear projections dominate compute and energy cost. Post-training quantization (PTQ) reduces this cost by mapping weights and activations to low-bit integers. Power-of-Two (PoT) weight quantization promises efficient multiplier-free LLM inference via shift-add logic, but suffers significant accuracy degradation at low bitwidths due to its coarse base-2 quantization grid. We introduce Fractional PoT (FPoT), a base-$\sqrt{2}$ (half-octave) fractional PoT grid that achieves practical 4-bit weight quantization while preserving a fully integer shift-add datapath. The method combines (i) the base-$\sqrt{2}$ weight grid with uniform integer activations, (ii) a calibration-time grid-alignment refinement absorbed into the static per-channel scale, and (iii) a lightweight 1-adder $\sqrt{2}$ approximation whose error is absorbed during weight calibration via approximate-grid quantization, yielding a processing element (PE) with significantly fewer gates than a 4-bit integer multiplier. On Llama-3-8B with 4-bit weight and activation quantization, FPoT improves zero-shot commonsense reasoning performance by 8.72% relative to the PoT baseline, while maintaining performance close to the uniform integer baseline, with only a negligible 0.4%p degradation. These results hold across various configurations spanning seven models and three bit-widths. Furthermore, FPoT can be integrated with state-of-the-art LLM PTQ methods, demonstrating framework-agnostic applicability.

cross Behavioral Compositionality of Sparse Autoencoder Representations

Authors: Haotian Ma, Ziye Mei, Frederic Sala

Abstract: Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) represent model activations with sparse, linearly decoded features, but the downstream effects of those features need not compose additively. We introduce SAE Additivity Residuals (SAR), which measure the behavioral variation left unexplained above a chosen interaction order. Under independent feature subset sampling, population SAR is exactly the normalized energy of interactions above that order and bounds coalition ordering error for the optimal low order approximation. To distinguish compositionality associated with the SAE representation from that induced by downstream computation, we compare learned SAE coordinates with sum preserving random rotations of the same selected subspace and find lower first order residuals for the learned coordinates across all 18 model and behavior settings. The coordinates are nevertheless not fully additive: pairwise terms improve held out coalition ordering in every setting and outperform parameter matched models using randomly selected third order terms. In a combined steering experiment, pairwise modeling raises rank fidelity from 0.5874 to 0.7790 and reduces ordering error from 0.1444 to 0.0606. Together, these results show that SAE coordinates support comparatively compositional behavioral descriptions while faithful joint explanations and interventions still require interactions beyond individual feature effects. Code is available at https://github.com/OpenSAE/SAE-Behavioral-Compositionality.

cross Loud and Clear: Making Visual Evidence Count in Multimodal Document RAG

Authors: Eunchan Jo, Seounghyun Jang, Sangwoo Mo

Abstract: Multimodal document retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) has focused largely on retrieval, yet its answers depend on how well a vision-language model reads the retrieved pages. cross Does On-Policy Distillation Need to Complete Every Rollout?

Authors: Zhengxin Zhang, Maosen Tang, Ning Wang, Changhao Li, Jingren Zhou, Bo Dai, Sainyam Galhotra, Alex Townsend, Claire Cardie

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) effectively transfers capabilities from a teacher to a student through feedback on student-generated rollouts. However, generating complete student rollouts and computing teacher feedback for every token make OPD computationally expensive. We introduce Stochastic Truncated On-Policy Distillation (STOPD), which accelerates OPD by randomly truncating student rollouts and correcting the resulting sampling bias with blockwise inverse-probability weighting (IPW). At each training step, we sample a stopping position for each rollout before generation and let the student generate until that position or natural termination. We obtain teacher feedback on every generated token and use blockwise IPW to compensate for the lower probability of retaining later tokens while controlling the weights. We prove that this weighting preserves Vanilla OPD's gradient sum in expectation and derive one-step update-error bounds for SGD, Adam, and Muon. We evaluate STOPD across multiple teacher-student model pairs, task domains, and optimizers. STOPD reduces generated tokens by 25-48% while maintaining accuracy comparable to Vanilla OPD, achieving 1.3-2.2x training speedups.

Abstract: Memory enables large language models (LLMs) to use information beyond their parameters, such as past user conversations, long documents, or an agent's earlier actions. cross PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems

Authors: Han Shen, Qifu Tyler Sun, Fanyang Meng, Ye Wang, Yongsheng Liang

Abstract: Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly deployed at the edge to support complex tasks that require collaboration and information exchange among multiple agents. However, when task contexts are passed across agents, shared context that has already been processed by upstream agents often needs to be prefilled again, resulting in substantial redundant computation. Reusing an already prefilled key--value (KV) cache can avoid this recomputation, but requires the reusable KV state to be transferred across the network before the receiving agent can begin generation. Under strict time-to-first-token (TTFT) deadlines and unreliable edge links, delivering the complete KV cache may be infeasible, making it necessary to prioritize and protect the most valuable KV information within a limited transmission budget. This paper proposes PriorityFEC, a utility-aware KV-cache handoff scheme that jointly exploits model-side KV importance and transport-side reliability. PriorityFEC first constructs a utility-aware source stream by placing high-utility KV blocks at earlier transmission positions. It then protects the ordered stream using a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism, which schedules repair packets according to receiver feedback before the TTFT deadline. We implement PriorityFEC on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype and evaluate it with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets. Results show that PriorityFEC achieves superior task quality over the compared transport schemes, with its advantages becoming particularly pronounced under stringent deadline constraints. These results demonstrate the importance of jointly optimizing KV-cache utility and deadline-aware transport reliability when reusable LLM state must be handed off across unreliable edge networks.

Abstract: Recent studies have reported promising results from quantum machine learning in drug discovery, healthcare, materials science and cybersecurity. cross Multi-Reward Hacking: From Aggregation-Induced Drift to Trust-Anchored Defense

Authors: Yushu Li, Jiin Woo, Xiaoxiao Li, Youngsuk Park

Abstract: To improve task performance while encouraging desirable response properties, LLM post-training often combines a primary reward (e.g., correctness) with auxiliary rewards (e.g., brevity or efficiency). However, how these rewards are aggregated can critically affect training outcomes. We show that symmetric aggregation, which treats reward channels equally, can induce a failure where aggregate reward improves while primary-task performance degrades, even when individual rewards are meaningful. We investigate this aggregation-induced failure through a one-step multi-reward decision formulation, characterizing when it constitutes multi-reward hacking. We prove that, under symmetric aggregation, deterministic conditional-mean GRPO converges to auxiliary-favoring behavior when auxiliary rewards are more reliably obtained, regardless of initialization. We propose Trust-Anchored Gating (TAG), which first elects an anchor channel based on the policy drift induced by optimizing each channel alone, then gates auxiliary credit on anchor satisfaction. Unlike calibration-sensitive static reweighting, TAG structurally eliminates credit for auxiliary-only outcomes while preserving additional credit for joint success. In the same analytical setting, TAG provably reverses this selection, converging to the primary-favoring action from any initialization when the primary channel is anchored. Experiments across Qwen and Llama models on mathematics and program synthesis reveal substantial degradation under symmetric, constrained, and normalized aggregation, whereas TAG mitigates this failure. On Qwen3-4B with a brevity reward, TAG achieves 0.581 pass@4 versus 0.220 for additive aggregation, while shortening responses by 44% relative to correctness-only training.

Authors: Yogesh Kumar

Abstract: Understanding video content requires reasoning at multiple levels of granularity, from identifying objects to interpreting their interactions and localizing when specific actions occur. cross V-SHARE: Provable Convergence Guarantees for Advantage-based Algorithms

Authors: Kintan Saha, Hamed Masoudi, Xinyi Sheng, Dominik Baumann, Joni Pajarinen, Arsenii Mustafin

Abstract: In reinforcement learning, advantage-based value-learning methods, unlike standard Q-learning, share information across actions by decomposing the action-value function into a state-dependent value component and action-specific advantages. While this mechanism has proven effective in both tabular and deep reinforcement learning, its theoretical understanding remains incomplete. We introduce V-SHARE, an advantage-based value-learning algorithm that combines cross-action value sharing with an importance-weighted Bellman-residual correction. To our knowledge, we provide the first complete theoretical characterization of an advantage-based algorithm, including both asymptotic convergence and non-asymptotic finite-sample guarantees. We identify a regime in which V-SHARE mitigates the rare-action bottleneck of asynchronous Q-learning and uncover a contraction--variance tradeoff between stronger deterministic contraction and increased importance-weighting noise. Motivated by this tradeoff, we develop decreasing residual-preconditioning schedules that combine faster early contraction with lower asymptotic noise. We further develop a two-timescale analysis that removes the single-timescale residual-weight threshold while preserving convergence guarantees. Experiments on discrete Markov decision processes (MDPs) and deep reinforcement-learning benchmarks corroborate our results.

cross Can Visual Generation Expand What Unified Multimodal Models Know?

Authors: Wenkai Li, Wenyi Hong, Zhuoyi Yang, Jie Tang

Abstract: Unified multimodal models (UMMs) receive image-generation supervision that exposes them to visual information not fully documented in text.
