replace Task-Level Natural Language Priors as Learning Signals for Low-Resource LLM Training

Authors: Jian Gao, Xiao Zhang, Xun Zhu, Miao Li, Ji Wu

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) often struggle when low-resource training data are ambiguous or incomplete. Task-level natural-language priors can provide useful guidance in such settings, but existing approaches usually treat these priors as input context rather than as learning signals during training. We study how task-level natural-language priors can supply guidance missing from these data through Prior-Guided Tuning (PGT). Its implementation, Contrastive Prior Steering (CPS), retains the supervised objective and adds positive- and negative-prior-conditioned losses with opposing optimization directions. Across AmbiMath, Diagnostic Anchoring, and Jigsaw, CPS achieves near-perfect exact-match accuracy on the synthetic task, improves accuracy under misleading diagnostic suggestions by 8.2 percentage points, and improves average subgroup Macro F1 on Jigsaw by 10.7 percentage points over plain fine-tuning, averaged over the two evaluated models. On Jigsaw, with one tenth of the experimental training data, CPS slightly exceeds full-data plain fine-tuning. These results show how reusable task-level language can complement limited labeled examples without requiring instance-level rationales or preference pairs.

replace Constructing and Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Trajectories for Medical Agent

Authors: Yunqi Zhu, Wen Tang, Jinghao Niu, Wensheng Zhang, Xuebing Yang

Abstract: Evaluation of medical artificial intelligence agents remains predominantly answer-centric, assessing only the correctness of final outputs while overlooking the quality of intermediate reasoning. In clinical settings, however, a correct answer reached through fabricated evidence or incoherent logic is as dangerous as an incorrect one. We propose MedTraj, a framework that treats reasoning trajectories as critical objects for construction, evaluation, and optimization. The pipeline generates structured multi-step clinical reasoning chains from medical diagnosis sources. Each trajectory is then parsed into clinical observations, evidence, numbered reasoning steps, and a final conclusion, and scored across five quality dimensions: coherence, evidence support, hallucination, completeness, and traceability. Controlled error injection introduces targeted faults into otherwise correct trajectories to establish causal links between specific reasoning failures and measurable quality degradation. Building on this, step-level filtering based on marginal contribution identifies which individual reasoning steps drive or undermine trajectory quality. Finally, quality-weighted context learning feeds trajectory evaluations back into the model at inference time, allowing it to learn from both strong and weak reasoning demonstrations. Experiments across CareQA, PubMedQA, and CECMed demonstrate that trajectory context consistently improves reasoning coherence, with gains of +0.029 to +0.041 over a zero-shot baseline. On the CECMed severity assessment task, quality-weighted context nearly doubles the correctness over the zero-shot baseline while cutting the hallucination ratio by 87%. Marginal-contribution analysis further shows that a small minority (15.3%) of reasoning steps carry most of the quality signal, and that extending chains beyond four steps yields diminishing returns.

replace CABAL: Multi-Agent Simulacra for Tracing the Effects of Collusive Bidding in Peer Review

Authors: Jicheng Zhou, Kemou Li, Kahim Wong, Zheyuan Li, Zhuan Shi, Fengpeng Li, Haiwei Wu, Jiantao Zhou

Abstract: Recent reports during the AAAI-27 review cycle highlight the risk of reviewers coordinating bids for reciprocal assignment advantage. Prior work treats bidding, reviewer assignment, and review manipulation as separate stages, leaving the lifecycle effects of collusive bidding unclear. Real-world analysis is further constrained by typically unobservable collusive intent and the lack of counterfactuals for the same conference. Motivated by this gap, we introduce \alg, an end-to-end multi-agent simulacra framework for studying reviewer assignment integrity by holding the conference environment fixed and configuring LLM-driven reviewer agents with honest or collusive policies. We further develop an affinity-guided collusive bidding strategy that uses mutual reviewer-paper affinities to construct collusion rings and select target papers, producing expertise-consistent rather than arbitrarily targeted attacks. Controlled experiments show that collusive bidding more than doubles target-paper capture and that assigned colluders score target papers about two points higher than honest co-reviewers, while conference-wide effects remain comparatively modest. Evaluated bid-phase detectors provide only limited evidence of collusion: in a fixed-triplet detector stress test, native positive-bid graphs are confounded by benign affinity, while a Very-High-only diagnostic view enables precise but low-coverage local recovery.

replace From Simulated Citizens to Simulated Deliberation: Challenges in Representation and Interaction

Authors: Chaemin Jang, Junsik Min, Jaewoo Choi, Donggyu Lee, Haiin Lee, Yoonseo Choi, Junyoung Park, Namhee Kim, Hyunwoo Kim, Juho Kim, Nuri Kim, Jihee Kim

Abstract: Can LLM agents simulate how a real public would deliberate? For such simulations to be meaningful, agents must reflect the population's opinions, and their views must be shaped by what others say during discussion. We evaluate these requirements across six LLMs using national surveys, controlled deliberation experiments, and a real deliberative poll. Comparing census-grounded Korean personas against national surveys, we find that agents often produce overly concentrated opinions and fail to reliably reproduce demographic differences. During simulated deliberation, agents exchange arguments, respond to one another, and frequently change positions. Yet similar changes occur even when agents cannot see one another's contributions, while simply restating their starting positions sharply reduces movement. We further test whether these limitations persist in a real-world setting, using agents constructed from actual respondents in the 2017 Shin-Kori deliberative poll. Even with real respondent profiles, agents fail to reproduce the panel's initial opinion distribution or how its opinions change across survey waves. Our findings show that generating deliberative discussions, representing public opinion, and reproducing interaction-driven opinion change are distinct capabilities. These results suggest a different potential role for LLM deliberation: surfacing arguments and perspectives, rather than simulating a real public.

replace T-GADE: Thermodynamical Generative-AI-Driven Evolution of LLM Artifacts

Authors: Kyoko Ogawa, Naoki Mori

Abstract: Introducing large language models (LLMs) into evolutionary computation enables semantic generation of candidates, but the classical problem of maintaining population diversity remains. Inspired by the thermodynamical genetic algorithm (TDGA), we propose T-GADE (Thermodynamical Generative-AI-Driven Evolution of LLM Artifacts), an evolutionary computation framework extended through LLMs. T-GADE keeps the notions of a population and genetic operators, takes structured artifacts generated by an LLM as individuals, and implements crossover and mutation as LLM operators. It further introduces the HoxLM, an LLM that attempts self-repair of an individual after evaluation and before survival selection; two update schemes, generational and steady-state; and Fermi-type and Bose-type occupancy rules that define solution identity and whether duplicates are permitted. Survival selection is unified as minimization of the free energy $F_T=\langle E\rangle-TH$, and the selection temperature $T$ explicitly controls the balance between quality and diversity. We tested an implementation example aligned with Evolution of Heuristics (EoH) on the online bin-packing task, where the excess is the bin count in excess of the volume lower bound, relative to that bound. The HoxLM-equipped configuration reached lower excess with fewer total LLM calls than EoH. In the preregistered comparison at 400 total calls it won 9 of 10 seed-paired runs, with a median sealed excess of 0.836% against 3.912%, and it was also better in the mean and in the worst case. Temperature controls the spread of the retained population under both update schemes, and a small positive temperature improves training quality over the collapsed $T=0$ point in the generational Bose-type scheme. The evolved heuristics are shown as readable scoring rules.

replace Witness Overlap: Directional Provenance Inside Open-Weight Model Families

Authors: Siyuan Li, Haoxuan Zeng, Xin Luo, Fernando Jia, Florence Li, Zhengyang Geng, Zico Kolter, Tai Sing Lee, Tianqin Li

Abstract: Open-weight models are often released, fine-tuned, aligned, merged, and re-released, making provenance audits ask not only whether checkpoints are related, but also which checkpoint came first. Many existing model-provenance methods are designed for a base-known audit setting: given a victim or source model, they test whether a suspect model is related to it. Although these audits are framed as source-to-suspect tests, their underlying evidence is often symmetric, relying on representation similarity, weight similarity, behavioral fingerprints, or correlation statistics. Symmetric pairwise comparisons can detect relatedness, but they cannot by themselves orient relationship between checkpoints A and B. We therefore introduce a local geometric comparison: instead of comparing two checkpoints directly, we add a third same-family checkpoint as a witness and compare the geometry around each candidate endpoint. Direction is inferred by asking which candidate behaves more like a branching parent. Motivated by this idea, and by the empirically observed asymmetry between parent-anchored and child-anchored witness-overlap distributions, we propose Witness Overlap, a prompt-free, training-free white-box test for directional provenance. On 176 LLM checkpoints from 16 families, our one-witness test orients 95.3\% of parent-child decisions using Frobenius cosine. We further evaluate root identification, sibling discrimination, generalizations to VLM and diffusion families, and chain-structured ordering. The signal is robust to weight noise and sparse pruning, with a proposed SVD weight reduction variant showing greater robustness than Frobenius cosine.

replace From Migration to Calibration: Aligning Agents with Deployment Requirements

Authors: Yaxiao Liu (PwC China AI Center), Pengbo Liu (PwC China AI Center), Yiwen Liu (PwC China AI Center), Yihua Guan (PwC China AI Center), Jiaxing Song (Tsinghua University), Xingjian Li (PwC China AI Center)

Abstract: Deploying, migrating, or scaling an agent changes its model, support system, application, and users. Calibration should align the agent with destination requirements without requiring source imitation or universal superiority. We propose three standard families: basic capability and operational reachability; technical and professional precision, including terminology, units, reporting periods, program fidelity, and report dependencies; and user context covering roles, use cases, authorized values, permissions, and budgets. A versioned mapping distinguishes requirements to preserve, transform, add, or retire. Four instruments---benchmarks, positive/negative examples, skills, and role-conditioned cost/utility---supply tunable reward channels within a fixed acceptance contract. Consultants annotate methods, branches, bounded loops, checkpoints, relative comparisons, and report refinement; a registry records applicable evidence. Adaptation revises configurations or trains a separate controller or skill while freezing the foundation executor. Independent hard gates govern qualification; RL and preference methods optimize candidates without supplying factual truth. Qualification and held-out improvement over the unchanged target are separate outcomes. Supplied intent labels permit a limited paired recount: small prompt/retry gains coexist with regressions and failed gates. FinQA supplements match prior summaries and retain provenance conflicts. These observations motivate destination-specific evaluation but do not establish calibration efficacy. We specify matched-budget tests, clean reload, complete-denominator reporting, and dependency revalidation. This is a methodological proposal with partially reconciled pilot evidence; confirmatory validation and the greater-than-80% zero-shot task-context acceptance target remain outstanding.

replace CompMat-Bench: Benchmarking AI Agents for Computational Materials Science

Authors: Chenmu Zhang, Levi Felix, Jun-Jie Zhang, Xingfu Li, Xuelian Jiang, Tao Jiang, Subhendu Mishra, Xixi Qin, Boris Yakobson

Abstract: Evaluating AI agents on scientific research tasks is constrained by the time and resources required for the underlying experiments or calculations. In computational materials research, repeating the same expensive simulations across agents and trials can make evaluation impractical. We introduce CompMat-Bench, a benchmark of 94 tasks derived from recently published computational materials studies, each asking agents to complete a step toward achieving the study's scientific goal. We reproduce the research steps in advance and assess agents on preparing inputs and analyzing outputs for expensive simulations, so expensive simulations can be avoided during evaluation. The reproduced inputs and results serve as ground truth for grading agents with fixed rules, without an LLM judge. The benchmark supports four evaluation conditions: single tasks and workflows composed of related tasks, each with full or reduced methodological guidance. With full guidance on single tasks, agents based on three LLMs demonstrate the ability to complete individual materials research steps, with pass rates of 66.0-90.4% across 94 tasks. Both longer workflows and reduced guidance can limit agent performance, but in different ways for different agents: they lower the pass rates of the weaker agents, whereas the strongest agent falls only when a long workflow is combined with reduced guidance. Failure analysis attributes most failures to scientific errors rather than to errors in software usage. CompMat-Bench provides a basis for comparing agents on the steps of real materials research and for analyzing agent failure modes.

replace Global Coherence: When Every Agent Is Right and the Team Is Still Wrong - A Local-to-Global Semantic Foundation for Multi-Agent Collaboration

Authors: Xin Heng

Abstract: AI agents can each make locally valid decisions yet jointly produce an invalid result. We call this the global coherence problem: a failure of shared state, not merely of intelligence. Our Observation-Aliasing Impossibility Theorem gives the exact boundary. A policy can guarantee a valid action exactly when all worlds producing the same observation share an admissible action. If k indistinguishable worlds require pairwise-disjoint actions, the best randomized worst-case success is 1/k; more reasoning, roles, messages, or samples cannot recover the missing distinction. A stronger model can reason better within its context, but it cannot see beyond it. The smallest sufficient context is NP-hard to find, yet counterexample-guided refinement finds a sufficient one and recertifies it as rules change. We give local-to-global runtime semantics X = (H, C, G, F; D): topology H records overlapping scopes; category C governs state-changing actions; groupoid G keeps reversible translations; sheaf F tests whether local views glue into one world; and history D keeps only what alters legal futures. Each preserves the needed information as state is split, translated, or changed. Models propose; the harness owns shared state and governs commit. Ten studies test the boundary. On a revision benchmark, a frontier model scores 40/40 when the deciding event is visible; hidden, every arm is consistent with chance (1/3); one restored fact returns 40/40. On TeamBench, ordinary teams exceed a shared budget in 5/5 runs, a visible live count leaves 4/5, and commit enforcement leaves 0/5. In tau2-bench Telecom, current-state checks score 0.07 after silent reverts; the harness scores 1.00. Refinement keeps a sufficient context through rule changes at 28% of the all-field cost. Solvers that already own the state tie it, as predicted. Local intelligence cannot substitute for missing global state.

replace KV$^2$: A Self-Refining KV Cache

Authors: Johannes Wesch, Danni Liu, Jan Niehues

Abstract: The memory footprint of the key-value (KV) cache constrains the practical use of long-context models, and it dominates cost when one prefilled context must later serve many different queries. In this reusable setting, query-agnostic compression trades cost against quality: lightweight estimators are cheap but less accurate, whereas full-context reconstruction scoring is more accurate yet reprocesses the entire prompt. We introduce KV$^2$, a query-agnostic KV-cache compression method based on selective reconstruction. KV$^2$ first uses a lightweight proxy scorer to identify informative in-context tokens, then reprocesses only this subset to compute final eviction scores. On RULER, Needle-in-a-Haystack, and LongBench, KV$^2$'s margin over baselines widens as the budget tightens: on RULER 16K at a 2% KV-cache budget it improves the average score over the next-best baseline by more than 40 percentage points, and on LongBench it attains the highest average across 2%-10% budgets at lower compression-stage runtime and peak memory than full-context reconstruction. Reusable KV-cache compression thus does not require reprocessing the full context. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KVsquared-0B97.

URLs: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/KVsquared-0B97.

replace Video-Time Priors for EEG-fNIRS Emotion Regression on Familiar Videos

Authors: Minghao Kong, Ying Gao, Jiurun Chen, Muyao Chen, Zheng Ge, Jianxin Wu, Rongjie Wang

Abstract: Do viewer-specific physiological signals improve continuous emotion regression beyond the stimulus-driven response shared across viewers? We examine this question by predicting emotion ratings for new viewers watching videos that also appear in the training set. As a stimulus-only baseline, we define a video-time prior:the smoothed median of training labels for each video at each second. We then combine this prior, using a fixed fusion weight, with a graph-based model of electroencephalography (EEG) and functional near-infrared spectroscopy (fNIRS) signals. On MER-PS 2026, five-fold cross-validation grouped by participant over 24 participants yields a mean absolute error (MAE) of 29.01 for fusion, compared with 29.06 for the prior alone. On an external cohort of four participants excluded from training, the corresponding MAEs are 27.72 and 28.04. In this cohort, most of the MAE reduction relative to a global-constant baseline comes from the prior, that is, from video identity and playback time alone.Fusion lowers MAE for three of the four held-out participants and for nine of the fifteen videos. The added value of physiological signals is therefore small and varies across participants and videos. Because the model uses physiological signals recorded after each prediction time, it supports only offline prediction. Generalization to unseen videos remains untested.

replace Asynchrony Is Nearly Free for Evolution Strategies on Long-Horizon LLM Agentic Tasks

Authors: William Hoy, Jingxuan Fan, Nurcin Celik, Xu Pan

Abstract: Synchronous policy updates can limit the efficiency of post-training for long-horizon LLM agents. When trajectories span multiple interaction turns, completion times can vary substantially, and synchronous update barriers leave faster workers idling, wasting compute resources. Asynchronous reinforcement learning addresses this inefficiency but introduces policy lag and off-policy optimization. Evolution strategies (ES) are a backpropagation-free alternative to gradient-based training, yet require many perturbed-policy evaluations and have remained largely synchronous in current LLM post-training. We introduce bounded-staleness asynchronous ES and investigate its training behavior under stale fitness measurements. On WebShop, we systematically vary the lag and fraction of stale fitness measurements and additionally introduce two stress tests to probe extreme staleness cases. Natural asynchrony closely tracks synchronous ES, and degradation in evaluation accuracy remains small even under extreme staleness, including when all fitness measurements are stale. These findings suggest that ES can tolerate substantial policy staleness without explicit off-policy correction, supporting its use in asynchronous post-training for long-horizon LLM agents.

replace OTel: Open Telco AI Datasets, Benchmarks, and Models

Authors: Farbod Tavakkoli, Gregory Diamos, Kenneth Church, Roderic Paulk, Jorden Terrazas, David Kanter, Mark Austin, Imtiaz Karim, Syed Ali Raza Zaidi, Lina Bariah, Merouane Abdelkader Debbah, Ali Maatouk, Louis Powell, Nikolaos Vasiloglou, Ashish Vaswani, Somanshu Singla, Adarsh Chaluvaraju

Abstract: We present Open Telco (OTel), an open telecom AI resource that releases derived telecom datasets for retrieval, reranking, instruction tuning, and safety/abstention, together with 30 full-parameter post-trained baselines spanning 10 embedding models, 3 rerankers, and 17 language models. The community has already engaged substantially with the resource: as of May 3, 2026, the released models have been downloaded over 16 million times and the project has received 157+ pieces of media coverage worldwide. Building on prior open telecom datasets and benchmarks, OTel provides documented telecom data sources, held-out evaluation partitions, trained embedding models, rerankers, context-grounded LLMs, and safety/abstention data in one unified resource. Each baseline starts from an open-weight model and is post-trained on OTel-derived data using an open training recipe, then evaluated on held-out OTel evaluation partitions. OTel post-training improves performance across all three model families: embedding retrieval reaches 93.1% NDCG@10, reranking reaches 0.947 MRR@10, and language-model correctness reaches 87.8%. We release OTel as a reproducible starting point and invite the community to expand the data, improve embedding and reranking models, and build stronger context-grounded telecom LLMs.

replace SCOPE: Certified Theorem Proving with a Language Model as the Policy Planner

Authors: Hanchao Zhou, Jialei Li

Abstract: In proof assistants such as Lean, a generated proof must pass machine compilation checks, so evaluation needs no human scoring. Direct generation fails on multi-step numeric propositions: a proof is valid only if every content integer is correct, so the pass rate is bounded by the k-th power of the per-integer accuracy. Controlled corruption across 2,617 reference proofs confirms this power law. SCOPE (State-Conditioned Operator Planning and Execution) enforces the natural division of labor: the model plans over an operator vocabulary, a symbolic engine executes the numerics, and a compiler renders the proof. On a 218-problem suite it certifies 191/218 (87.6%) with a 135M backbone; the 7B DeepSeek-Prover-V1.5-RL certifies 18/218 at 27.5 times the tokens and 37.5 times the wall-clock, and DeepSeek-Prover-V2-7B certifies zero on a bidirectional dual suite. Multi-step thinking costs 6.12 discrete decision actions per problem and produces no natural-language thinking text. Replacing the lagged engine state in the decision frame with the current one lifts the pass rate from 117/218 to 191/218, while up-weighting the chain-end loss hurts. On the public Lean-Workbook library, 2,132 of 3,536 gradeable admissible problems certify (60.29%) with zero regression on the main suite. All readings come from a version-frozen review with independent rechecks and reverse verification. Restricting free generation and keeping decision-time information visible is a more direct route than enlarging the model.

replace ParanoiaEval: Benchmarking Unnecessary Defensive Work in Agentic Coding

Authors: Hanjun Luo, Xiucheng Zhang, Zhuoning Xu, Zhimu Huang, Yingbin Jin, Xinfeng Li, Hanan Salam

Abstract: As coding agents increasingly undertake real-world work autonomously, judging whether their risk treatments are warranted has become important. Existing work evaluates related agent behaviors from separate perspectives, but lacks a systematic framework for unifying these behaviors. To bridge this gap, we introduce ParanoiaEval, the first benchmark for unified evaluation of risk-treatment capabilities in coding agents. Grounded in the well-established Avoidance-Transfer-Mitigation-Acceptance framework in software engineering risk management, ParanoiaEval operationalizes its 4 fundamental treatments for coding-agent settings and contains 200 evidence-controlled repository-level task pairs, each differing only in treatment-defining evidence. We further introduce dedicated metrics for risk-treatment violations and evidence responsiveness, using a human-calibrated agentic judge for reliable evaluation. Large-scale experiments on 8 representative models and a post-hoc human study reveal that (I) unnecessary risk treatment occurs in 11.2%-58.7% of runs despite explicit evidence, with substantial variation across agent configurations; (II) stronger task capability does not ensure more appropriate risk treatment, while treatment violations substantially harm developers' experience, establishing risk treatment as an independent capability dimension; and (III) agents exhibit systematic patterns consistent with established risk-management findings, suggesting that knowledge from human practice can guide the diagnosis and improvement of this capability.

replace AlphaRLM: Aligning Language Models for Literature-Grounded Symbolic Factor Discovery

Authors: Jie Huang, Yuecheng Hong, Yao Chen, Hao Zhong, Linfeng Xiao, Xudong Liu, Qiang Ye, Hao Xia, Jianxuan Xin

Abstract: Formulaic alpha mining seeks to discover executable expressions that capture predictive market signals in a vast, noisy search space. Recent LLM-based approaches use prompting, iterative search, or seed-factor evolution, leaving direct factor construction through policy learning less explored. To address this, we introduce AlphaRLM, a reinforcement-learning framework that trains LLMs to construct executable symbolic factors from financial literature without input factor expressions. AlphaRLM incorporates Literature-Guided Operator Assembly (LEGO), which conditions factor construction on literature-derived ideas and executable operators, providing semantic priors for exploration. It further aligns the generation policy with market feedback through executable-validity and grouped-return-separation rewards that capture non-monotonic structures across factor quantiles. Extensive experiments on CSI 300, CSI 1000, and S&P 500 show that AlphaRLM outperforms strong factor-mining and LLM-based baselines in annualized excess return. Notably, the 8B model achieves strong cross-market performance, highlighting the promise of literature-conditioned, market-aligned factor generation.

replace RAP-WAM: Learning World-Action Models through Predictive Rehearsal and Action Perturbations

Authors: Yuanpeng He, Ceyao Zhang, Fangjing Li, Kefei Zhu, Lijian Li, Yueheng Li, Tianjia He, Wenpin Jiao, Zhi Jin, Yaodong Yang, Yuanpei Chen

Abstract: Recent world action models draw on video generation priors to jointly predict future observations and robot actions. Adapting these priors to control requires determining how future predictions should guide policy learning. RAP-WAM combines predictive Rehearsal and Action Perturbations (RAP) with future-supervised queries and Coupled Action Projection for policy learning. Predictive rehearsal trains on model-generated inputs while retaining recorded targets, and accumulates recurring errors into a spatial prior. We perturb actions and measure changes in video predictions. Action-response-based spatiotemporal credit assignment combines these responses, prediction error, and the rehearsal prior to allocate visual supervision; spatially aggregating the responses determines supervision intensity across future action steps. No separate scoring network or deployment-time probe is required. The base learns from a video-pretrained backbone without intermediate pretraining on external embodied data. We evaluate the fixed base and its agent-refined, task-conditioned system. On 42 RoboDojo tasks, their average success rates are 12.85% and 15.62%, with Scores of 17.86 and 20.59. Across 15 real-robot tasks, the complete system achieves 89.00% task-macro-average success, exceeding the strongest of five baselines by 37.67 percentage points. Across 315 fixed-output simulation windows, 45.21% of response-top20 positions fall outside error-top20, revealing information beyond prediction error. Videos and further details are available on the project website: https://rapwam.github.io/.

URLs: https://rapwam.github.io/.

replace LIBERO-Industry: Benchmarking Robotic Manipulation under Industrial Process Constraints

Authors: Ming Shin Cindy Sung, Duan Leijiahao, Haoxiang Wang, Boning Tan, Liangna Fu, Chao Tan, Yafu Li, Yulong Chen, Chaoqun Wan, Zhen Huang, Shuxian Liang, Xian-Sheng Hua

Abstract: Industrial manipulation in flexible manufacturing, e.g., high-mix, low-volume (HMLV) electronics assembly, requires robot policies to adapt to changing products and production conditions. Such settings impose industrial process constraints, including product variants, moving material flows, finite execution windows, and tight geometric tolerances. However, existing evaluation frameworks do not systematically capture these constraints when assessing generalist robot policies. To address this gap, we introduce LIBERO-Industry, a novel benchmark built on the widely used robot learning benchmark LIBERO, that translates the aforementioned industrial constraints into controllable task conditions and measurable success criteria. Specifically, LIBERO-Industry comprises three classic task scenarios: Static-to-Dynamic (S2D) loading, Dynamic-to-Static (D2S) unloading, and Dynamic-to-Dynamic (D2D) transfer. The suites contain 216 tasks in total that systematically vary object geometry, conveyor speed, interaction-window duration, and geometric acceptance region size. Extensive experiments across five generalist robot policies (including vision-language-action models and world-action models) show that performance progressively degrades as industrial constraints are introduced and combined. Under a shared fine-tuning and evaluation protocol, the LIBERO-Industry co-training condition achieved higher observed target-transfer completion than the original-LIBERO condition.

replace ReTerm: Recovery Training for Self-Improving Terminal Agents

Authors: Wei Xie, Kaituo Feng, Shiming Fu, Yong Liu, Xiangyu Yue, Shuangjia Zheng

Abstract: Terminal agents have shown strong abilities in complex coding tasks. However, they often struggle to recover from errors during long-running execution (e.g., command errors, faulty implementations), eventually leading to task failures. To this end, we present ReTerm, a self-improving terminal agent trained by learning how to recover from its own failed terminal tasks. To support its training, we construct a customized data pipeline and produce ReTerm-10K, a high-quality dataset that provides effective recovery demonstrations for SFT cold start. Furthermore, we propose ReTerm-GRPO, an online RL algorithm that forks from the agent's own failure states to generate multiple independent self-recovery attempts. Built on Qwen3.5-9B, our ReTerm-9B achieves 34.8% Pass@1 on Terminal-Bench 2.0, improving over the base model by 14.6 points. Further analysis shows that our method achieves consistently higher recovery rates across diverse error categories compared with standard GRPO training. All code, data and model are released at https://github.com/twothsnd/ReTerm.

replace What Coding Agents Leave Unsaid: Measuring Task-State Gaps Between Execution and Reports

Authors: Guoyang Xu, Rui Zhang, Xiuyu Wu, Hao Chen

Abstract: Human supervisors rely on coding agents' reports to assess progress, verify repairs, and decide when to intervene. But a supervisor cannot investigate a failure they were never told occurred. Failed attempts, workarounds, and unresolved risks may disappear from routine reports, leaving supervisors unaware of issues that warrant scrutiny. We conduct CTXGAP, a measurement study quantifying the gap in task-state recoverability between agents' accumulated user-facing reports and their full recorded execution contexts. Across 37 coding tasks and 4,799 valid probes, agents answer 93.6% of task-state questions on demand; full-context readers recover 92.7%, while report-only readers recover 52.0%, a 40.7-point gap. At the final checkpoints of 30 verified fixes, the gap remains 34.8 points. Human annotation identifies states mentioned without enough supporting detail as the largest category of unrecovered information, and model-assessed supervision-relevant states retain substantial gaps. Full terminal scrollback improves recovery to 73.6% but still trails full context. Under matched report lengths, context-informed rewrites improve recovery by about eight points, without a detectable targeted-over-natural advantage. Positive gaps persist across harnesses and independent DeepSeek execution and measurement. CTXGAP establishes report-based task-state recoverability as a complementary evaluation dimension.

replace Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents

Authors: Haofei Yu, Yixing Chen, Yining Zhao, Xuyan Ye, Yuxiang Liu, Zhigang Hua, Yan Xie, Shuang Yang, Jiaxuan You

Abstract: Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $\tau^2$-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.

URLs: https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.

replace-cross Distributionally Robust Regularization of Sparse Integer Programming Trained Learning Models

Authors: Sanjeeb Dash, Soumyadip Ghosh, Joao Goncalves, Mark S. Squillante

Abstract: Building explainable machine learning models is crucial for human users to be able to interpret the proposed statistical relationship obtained from the training data. Mixed-integer optimization formulations are often used to train such models with explicit sparsity constraints, aiming to strike the right trade-off between sparsity and prediction accuracy. Existing methods to find the right choice of sparsity -- e.g., via cross-validation -- are computationally expensive. For convex model training formulations, recent advances in distributionally robust optimization (DRO) provide strong generalization while sidestepping this computational burden. We describe an extension of such regularization via DRO to mixed-integer sparse programs, providing statistical guarantees as a function of an associated sparsity parameter of the formulation. We illustrate the use of this approach in the case of building explainable binary classification models using sets of feature value rules.

replace-cross InkSight: Offline-to-Online Handwriting Conversion by Teaching Vision-Language Models to Read and Write

Authors: Blagoj Mitrevski, Arina Rak, Julian Schnitzler, Chengkun Li, Andrii Maksai, Jesse Berent, Claudiu Musat

Abstract: Digital note-taking is gaining popularity, offering a durable, editable, and easily indexable way of storing notes in a vectorized form, known as digital ink. However, a substantial gap remains between this way of note-taking and traditional pen-and-paper note-taking, a practice that is still favored by a vast majority. Our work InkSight, aims to bridge the gap by empowering physical note-takers to effortlessly convert their work (offline handwriting) to digital ink (online handwriting), a process we refer to as derendering. Furthermore, it generalizes beyond its training domain into simple sketches. replace-cross Pairwise Difference Learning for Classification

Authors: Mohamed Karim Belaid, Maximilian Rabus, Eyke H\"ullermeier

Abstract: Pairwise difference learning (PDL) has recently been introduced as a new meta-learning technique for regression. Instead of learning a mapping from instances to outcomes in the standard way, the key idea is to learn a function that takes two instances as input and predicts the difference between the respective outcomes. Given a function of this kind, predictions for a query instance are derived from every training example and then averaged. This paper extends PDL toward the task of classification and proposes a meta-learning technique for inducing a PDL classifier by solving a suitably defined (binary) classification problem on a paired version of the original training data. We analyze the performance of the PDL classifier in a large-scale empirical study and find that it outperforms state-of-the-art methods in terms of prediction performance. Last but not least, we provide an easy-to-use and publicly available implementation of PDL in a Python package.

replace-cross LLMR: Knowledge Distillation with a Large Language Model-Induced Reward

Authors: Dongheng Li, Yongchang Hao, Lili Mou

Abstract: Large language models have become increasingly popular and demonstrated remarkable performance in various natural language processing (NLP) tasks. However, these models are typically computationally expensive and difficult to be deployed in resource-constrained environments. In this paper, we propose LLMR, a novel knowledge distillation (KD) method based on a reward function induced from large language models. We conducted experiments on multiple datasets in the dialogue generation and summarization tasks. Empirical results demonstrate that our LLMR approach consistently outperforms traditional KD methods in different tasks and datasets.

replace-cross STAMP: Symmetric Temporal Averaging Message Passing for Domain Adaptation in Chronologically Split Graphs

Authors: Joo Young Park, Sejun Park, Hyunwoo Park

Abstract: This paper addresses domain adaptation in graph-structured data under chronological splits. We consider transductive semi-supervised node classification (SSNC), in which labels of historical nodes are used to predict labels of more recent nodes. Temporal dependencies in graph connectivity induce distribution shifts across time, often degrading the performance of models trained on past nodes. Despite its practical relevance, domain adaptation under chronological graph splits remains relatively underexplored. We propose Symmetric Temporal Averaging Message Passing (STAMP), a timestamp-balanced operator that replaces standard neighborhood aggregation with temporally aligned propagation. We call its specialized variant for the heterogeneous ogbn-mag dataset STAMP-mag, reserving STAMP for the general formulation. By balancing messages across timestamps, STAMP enforces first-moment invariance across temporal domains while remaining scalable and easy to implement. Unlike many existing domain adaptation methods, whose assumptions are difficult to verify on graphs, STAMP relies on transparent, practically checkable conditions and is supported by theoretical analysis. Empirically, STAMP and STAMP-mag improve accuracy across all evaluated temporal graph benchmarks. STAMP achieves state-of-the-art performance on ogbn-arxiv, while STAMP-mag does so on ogbn-mag. For temporal-split Pokec, where no established benchmark exists, applying STAMP to the state-of-the-art model for the original dataset consistently improves performance under our chronological split. These results demonstrate that timestamp-balanced propagation is an effective and practical approach to mitigating temporal distribution shift in graph learning.

replace-cross A self-learning framework for adaptive free-form pipe routing in aeroengines

Authors: Caicheng Wang, Zili Wang, Shuyou Zhang, Yongzhe Xiang, Zheyi Li, Jianrong Tan

Abstract: Pipe routing is a highly complex, time-consuming, and no-deterministic polynomial-time hard (NP-hard) problem in aeroengine design. Despite extensive research efforts in optimizing constant-curvature pipe routing, the growing demand for free-form pipes poses new challenges. Dynamic design environments and fuzzy layout rules further impact the optimization performance and efficiency. To tackle these challenges, this study proposes a self-learning-based method (SLPR) for optimizing free-form pipe routing in aeroengines. The SLPR is based on the proximal policy optimization (PPO) algorithm and integrates a unified rule modeling framework for efficient obstacle detection and fuzzy rule modeling in continuous space. Additionally, a potential energy table is constructed to enable rapid queries of layout tendencies and interference. The agent within SLPR iteratively refines pipe routing and accumulates the design knowledge through interaction with the environment. Once the design environment shifts, the agent can swiftly adapt by fine-tuning network parameters. Comparative tests reveal that SLPR ensures smooth pipe routing through cubic non-uniform B-spline (NURBS) curves, avoiding redundant pipe segments found in constant-curvature pipe routing. Results in both static and dynamic design environments demonstrate that SLPR outperforms three representative baselines in terms of the pipe length reduction, the adherence to layout rules, the path complexity, and the computational efficiency. Furthermore, tests in dynamic environments indicate that SLPR eliminates labor-intensive searches from scratch and even yields superior solutions compared to the retrained model. These results highlight the practical value of SLPR for real-world pipe routing, meeting lightweight, precision, and sustainability requirements of the modern aeroengine design.

replace-cross Towards Reliable Long-Context Reasoning: Detecting Unanswerable Questions via FactGuard

Authors: Qian-Wen Zhang, Biao Liu, Fang Li, Jie Wang, Lingfeng Qiao, Yifei Yu, Di Yin, Xing Sun

Abstract: Despite the success of Large Language Models (LLMs) in long-context tasks, their reliability in recognizing unanswerable questions remains a critical challenge. In information-seeking scenarios, models often suffer from overconfidence and hallucinations when evidence is missing or misleading. In this paper, we propose FACTGUARD, a systematic multi-task workflow for generating evidence-grounded answerable and unanswerable questions. We introduce FACTGUARD-BENCH, a benchmark comprising 25,220 examples with context lengths up to 128K, specifically targeting Lack of Evidence and Misleading Evidence scenarios. Evaluations of eleven representative open-source and proprietary LLMs reveal a significant reliability gap: models struggle to reject unanswerable questions as context scales, with the leading model achieving only 71.62% accuracy. By training with FACTGUARD-BENCH, we improve overall accuracy to 81.17% and significantly enhance models' reasoned rejection capabilities. Our work provides a scalable path toward building more faithful and trustworthy long-context LLMs. Our source is publicly available at https://github.com/biaoliu-kiritsugu/FactGuard.

URLs: https://github.com/biaoliu-kiritsugu/FactGuard.

replace-cross PanoDiff-SR: Synthesizing Dental Panoramic Radiographs using Diffusion and Super-resolution

Authors: Sanyam Jain, Bruna Neves de Freitas, Andreas Basse-OConnor, Alexandros Iosifidis, Ruben Pauwels

Abstract: Interest in generating realistic synthetic medical images has grown in recent years. Such datasets can mitigate the scarcity of public data for artificial intelligence research and support education. Work on synthesizing dental panoramic radiographs (PRs) has so far relied almost exclusively on generative adversarial networks (GANs), and three limitations recur: the synthesized field of view is often restricted to a cropped dentoalveolar region rather than a complete PR; observer studies of GAN-generated PRs report that clinicians still identify synthetic images with accuracies of roughly 78-82%, implying persistent visible giveaways; and adversarial training remains sensitive to instability and mode collapse. Generating a full-size PR directly at diagnostic resolution with a diffusion model is an alternative, but sampling at that resolution is costly. We therefore present PanoDiff-SR, an end-to-end two-stage pipeline developed and evaluated specifically for panoramic radiography: a denoising diffusion implicit model (PanoDiff) generates a low-resolution (LR) seed PR ($256\times128$), which a fine-tuned hybrid attention transformer (HAT) super-resolution (SR) stage upscales to a high-resolution (HR) PR of $1024\times512$. Decoupling the expensive generative step from the resolution step yields complete, full-arch PRs at diagnostic resolution on a single 47 GB GPU. The Fr\'echet inception distance between 7243 real and synthetic HR images is 40.5. Inception scores were 2.68, 2.38, 2.90 and 2.98 for real HR, synthetic HR, real LR and synthetic LR images, respectively. Six clinical experts, each evaluating 100 synthetic and 100 real PRs under a time limit, distinguished real from synthetic images with an average accuracy of 68.5% (50% corresponds to random guessing), substantially closer to chance than reported for GAN-based studies. Project page: https://s4nyam.github.io/panodiff/

URLs: https://s4nyam.github.io/panodiff/

replace-cross Adaptive Duration Model for Text Speech Alignment

Authors: Junjie Cao

Abstract: Speech-to-text alignment is a critical component of neural text to speech (TTS) models. Autoregressive TTS models typically use an attention mechanism to learn these alignments on-line, while non-autoregressive end to end TTS models rely on durations extracted from external sources. In this paper, we propose a novel duration prediction framework that can give promising phoneme-level duration distribution with given text. In our experiments, the proposed duration model has more precise prediction and adaptation ability to conditions, compared to previous baseline models. replace-cross AI Scientists for Building Virtual-Cell Models

Authors: Xiangru Tang, Jiapeng Chen, Daniel Shao, Zhuoyun Yu, Yan Cui, Weixu Wang, Fang Wu, Yuchen Zhuang, Wenqi Shi, Zhi Huang, Arman Cohan, Xihong Lin, Fabian Theis, Smita Krishnaswamy, Mark Gerstein

Abstract: Autonomous agents, from virtual laboratories to biological data-analysis agents and co-scientists, have begun to turn scientific goals into hypotheses. Whether they can go further and convert real, noisy and complex biological data into executable model hypotheses and architectures that encode testable assumptions remains unclear. Predicting how cells respond to genetic and chemical perturbations is a natural test of this ability: it is central to decoding disease mechanisms and therapeutic discovery, yet it demands cross-disciplinary expertise rarely concentrated in a single research group. Here, we introduce CellForge, an organized team of autonomous agents for single-cell perturbation modeling. Given a dataset and a task, it surveys the literature, parses the data, identifies relevant baselines, and formulates a model hypothesis through critic-mediated discussion among agents specialized in data modeling, single-cell biology, and deep learning. It then writes the training code, runs the experiments, and returns a trained predictor including architecture, code, and validation results. The architecture itself is the hypothesis: CellForge produces it end-to-end, with no human specifying the design, loss, or training protocol. Across six perturbation datasets spanning transcriptomic, epigenomic, and proteomic contexts, CellForge yields models that are competitive with hand-crafted state-of-the-art results on established tasks and extends the same workflow to paired multi-omic readouts. Blinded human evaluators rate CellForge's research plans as more scientifically sound and innovative than those from single LLMs or deep-research systems. In summary, we show autonomous agents can complete the full hypothesis-to-validation cycle in single-cell biology, with an AI effectively building another AI, in a complex setting that currently requires a team of human experts.

replace-cross UniFGVC: Universal Training-Free Few-Shot Fine-Grained Vision Classification via Attribute-Aware Multimodal Retrieval

Authors: Haiyun Guo, Hongyu Guo, Xiangzhao Hao, Jiarui Guo, Jinqiao Wang, Tat-Seng Chua

Abstract: Few-shot fine-grained visual classification (FGVC) aims to discriminate between subtly different categories with very limited data. While Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLM) exhibit strong capabilities in both fine-grained visual understanding and open-world category recognition, directly fine-tuning them on few-shot FGVC tasks typically leads to overfitting on sparse samples, which erodes their valuable open-world knowledge and compromises generalization. To fundamentally overcome this limitation, we present UniFGVC, a universal training-free framework that shifts the paradigm from parameter fine-tuning to training-free retrieval-based inference. UniFGVC reframes few-shot FGVC as a multimodal retrieval problem using predefined category templates. Its core is the Category-Discriminative Visual Captioner (CDV-Captioner), an attribute-aware module designed to leverage MLLM for generating structured textual descriptions that explicitly pinpoint discriminative visual attributes between similar classes. By integrating chain-of-thought reasoning and visual references, CDV-Captioner mitigates hallucination and maximizes the discriminative power of textual captions, thereby converting images into attribute-enriched image-description pairs. Subsequently, utilizing arbitrary pre-trained visual and textual encoders in a plug-and-play manner, UniFGVC extracts features from both modalities and computes their similarity with all category templates for recognition. Extensive experiments on 12 FGVC datasets demonstrate UniFGVC's universal compatibility and consistent superiority. It not only outperforms existing few-shot CLIP-based methods but also surpasses several fully-supervised MLLM-based approaches, confirming the general advantage of the proposed retrieval paradigm.

replace-cross Drive As You Like: Multi-Head Diffusion with Reinforcement Learning for Personalized Driving

Authors: Ding Fan, Xuewen Luo, Hwa Hui Tew, Susilawati, Vishnu Monn Baskaran, Fucai Ke

Abstract: Personalized autonomous driving requires understanding the driving needs implicit in user requests. This is challenging when driving demonstrations lack style annotations and behavioral adaptation risks degrading planning quality. We propose M-Diffusion Planner with a dual-decoupling design: separating intent understanding from trajectory planning, and general planning capability learning from strategy alignment. A large language model infers driving needs and selects a strategy, which is executed by the corresponding head of a shared diffusion planner. Following imitation pretraining, strategy-specific rewards specialize individual heads without style annotations. We innovatively adapt Group Relative Policy Optimization using surrogate policy-ratio estimation and constrained head-specific updates to mitigate degradation of pretrained planning capabilities. Open-loop and closed-loop evaluations on nuPlan demonstrate distinct strategy-aligned behaviors and competitive planning performance. Further experiments assess intent-to-strategy mapping and collision risk, and analyze the effects of reward design and optimization on strategy alignment and planning quality.

replace-cross ZeST: A VLM-based Zero-Shot Traversability Navigation for Unknown Environments

Authors: Shreya Gummadi, Mateus V. Gasparino, Gianluca Capezzuto, Marcelo Becker, Girish Chowdhary

Abstract: Advances in robotics and autonomous navigation systems hinge on the ability to predict terrain traversability accurately. Traditional methods for generating datasets to train these prediction models often involve putting robots into potentially hazardous environments, posing risks to equipment and safety. To solve this problem, we present ZeST, a novel approach that treats repeated VLM outputs as stochastic measurements and fuses them into an uncertainty-aware posterior. Our approach not only performs zero-shot traversability and mitigates the risks associated with real-world data collection but also accelerates the development of advanced navigation systems, offering a cost-effective and scalable solution. To support our findings, we present navigation results, in both controlled indoor and unstructured outdoor environments. Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) excel at operating at scale by leveraging social media and data crawled from the web. replace-cross Uncovering Grounding IDs: How External Cues Shape Multi-Modal Binding

Authors: Amirmohammad Izadi, Hosein Hasani, Fatemeh Askari, Mobin Bagherian, Sadegh Mohammadian, Mohammad Izadi, Mahdieh Soleymani Baghshah

Abstract: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) perform well on multimodal tasks, but their ability to reason and precisely align visual and textual information still has room for improvement. In this study, we show that external visual cues, such as symbols or grid lines, help LVLMs form more accurate connections between visual components, such as objects, and their corresponding textual descriptions, improving their grounding and reasoning abilities. We introduce the concept of Grounding IDs, which are latent identifiers that arise within the model as a result of external cues structuring both visual and textual modalities. Our analysis reveals that partition-inducing external cues lead to Grounding IDs that make better alignment between corresponding visual and text representations, helping the model focus on relevant information. We find that Grounding IDs enhance attention between related components, improving cross-modal grounding and reducing hallucinations. Overall, our results show that Grounding IDs are a key mechanism that enables external cues to improve cross-modal alignment, reduce errors, and enhance the overall performance of LVLMs across a range of multimodal tasks.

replace-cross Multi-task Neural Diffusion Processes

Authors: Joseph Rawson, Domniki Ladopoulou, Petros Dellaportas

Abstract: Neural diffusion processes provide a scalable, non-Gaussian approach to modelling distributions over functions, but existing formulations are limited to single-task inference and do not capture dependencies across related tasks. We propose multi-task neural diffusion processes (MT-NDPs), an extension that incorporates a task encoder to enable task-conditioned probabilistic regression and few-shot adaptation across related functions. The task encoder extracts a low-dimensional representation from context observations and conditions the diffusion model on this representation, allowing information sharing across tasks while accommodating variable-sized context sets and preserving permutation invariance with respect to the ordering of context observations. We evaluate MT-NDPs through synthetic experiments and two real-world applications involving wind turbine data and school examination outcomes. The results demonstrate improved predictive accuracy relative to single-task diffusion baselines when context observations are available, alongside competitive performance against neural-process and Gaussian-process alternatives. In the wind-energy application, MT-NDPs achieve the lowest average coverage error among the evaluated models at larger context sizes, demonstrating improved predictive uncertainty calibration. Overall, our findings highlight the potential of task-conditioned diffusion models for uncertainty-aware multi-task regression and few-shot adaptation to previously unseen tasks.

replace-cross RAG4Tickets: AI-Powered Ticket Resolution via Retrieval-Augmented Generation on JIRA and GitHub Data

Authors: Mohammad Baqar

Abstract: Modern software teams frequently encounter delays in resolving recurring or related issues due to fragmented knowledge scattered across JIRA tickets, developer discussions, and GitHub pull requests (PRs). replace-cross Scientific feedback reveals productive regimes beyond training conventions

Authors: Yoon Pyo Lee, Samrendra Roy, Souvik Chakraborty, Syed Bahauddin Alam

Abstract: Human-imposed design conventions can restrict the alternatives represented in scientific training data and shape the search preferences learned by generative models. We show that scientific performance feedback can redirect the learned search distribution toward productive regions beyond those conventions, without explicitly rewarding departures from them. We test this process in reactor lattice design with simulated feedback and chemical reaction-condition selection with measured yields, using a pretrained sequence policy and a small non-language policy, respectively. In both settings, adaptation improves scientific performance while increasing generation of choices excluded from the corresponding supervised data. In measured reactions, probability on task-specific ligand choices excluded from training rises from 0.29 to 0.70-0.71 as expected yield error decreases. Controls in the lattice setting show that the productive region identified during adaptation provides reusable information to random search, while aligned generation produces useful candidates more frequently than informed random sampling. Removing the target penalty redirects four of five matched runs, linking the direction of adaptation to the scientific objective. Together, these results show how scientific feedback reshapes inherited search preferences and reveals productive alternatives beyond training conventions, with attribution controls distinguishing learned redistribution from candidate availability and reusable search information.

replace-cross Benchmarking Code LLM-as-a-Judge via Constrained Perturbation and Calibration

Authors: Ruiqi Wang, Xinchen Wang, Cuiyun Gao, Chun Yong Chong, Xin Xia, Qing Liao

Abstract: Code from LLM-assisted development may contain defects like API hallucinations, motivating the development of code evaluation metrics to assess such code before deployment. As conventional rule-based metrics merely score code based on similarity to reference programs, and execution-based metrics require expensive manual preparation for tests, researchers have developed LLM-as-a-judge metrics for both behavioral and nonbehavioral code evaluation, and curated multiple benchmarks to validate their effectiveness. Furthermore, they often directly prompt LLMs to generate code for evaluation, which may lead to unbalanced code quality distributions, failing to test the metrics' discriminative capability across varying quality levels. To curate a diverse benchmark with a balanced program quality distribution and streamline manual annotation, we propose AXIOM, a novel perturbation-based framework. It reframes program scores as the refinement effort needed for deployment, with two stages: (1) Quality-constrained semantic perturbation, prompting LLMs to apply sequences of predefined perturbation rules to existing programs, precisely controlling each program's target quality to achieve a balanced distribution. (2) Hybrid quality calibration, selecting semantically distinct programs for benchmark diversity, and performing streamlined manual annotation with multisource context. With AXIOM, we curate AXIOMBENCH, a large-scale multilingual benchmark with 1,962 code evaluation problems in a more balanced score distribution...

Abstract: Cardiovascular disease arises from interactions between inherited risk, molecular programmes, and tissue-scale remodelling that are observed clinically through imaging. replace-cross Contextual Probability and Operational Minimality in Tug-of-War Decision Making

Authors: Song-Ju Kim

Abstract: What connects minimal decision-making to contextual probability? We address this question through the Tug-of-War (TOW) principle, a resource-coupled mechanism for multi-armed bandit decision-making. Starting from classical probability, we show that when reward-learning functionality and specified information requirements are fixed, minimizing the necessary relaxation of common event semantics can lead to nonclassical probability. We derive the exact minimum semantic repair and identify the conditions under which information efficiency, calibration, and compositional consistency select contextual statistics. We then construct a single quantum Tug-of-War (QTOW) instrument in which the same decision--reward process both improves stationary decision performance and drives the unconditional learned state beyond the Klyachko--Can--Binicio\u{g}lu--Shumovsky (KCBS) noncontextuality bound. A finite diagnostic based on the instrument's recovery operations also yields a corrected contextuality witness. These results connect minimal decision-making, operational economy, and the emergence of contextual probability.

replace-cross Product Interaction: An Algebraic Formalism for Deep Learning Architectures

Authors: Haonan Dong, Chun-Wun Cheng, Angelica I. Aviles-Rivero

Abstract: Modern neural architectures are largely developed as distinct empirical constructions, despite recurring principles across convolutional, attention, state-space, and equivariant models. We introduce Product Interactions, an algebraic formalism that unifies these architectures under a common construction principle and turns their design into a systematic procedure. The core shift is representing data as algebraic elements and treating multiplication, rather than linear transformation alone, as a primitive architectural operation. Architectures are generated by composing multiplication operators, with input dependence organized by self-interaction order. Within this formalism, we derive convolutional and equivariant networks as symmetry-constrained first-order product interactions, recover Mamba from state-space models as an increase in self-interaction order from one to three, and establish attention as a third-order interaction from composing the same operator. Equivariance is incorporated by constraining the algebraic product via symmetry: translation equivariance yields convolution, while SO(3)-compatibility produces the Clebsch--Gordan product and tensor-field interactions. This perspective recasts architecture design as an algebraic construction problem: the choice of algebra defines admissible interactions, symmetry constrains their structure, and composition generates higher-order architectures. The framework is thus generative. Following these principles, we construct a simplified-algebra dynamical layer that improves both accuracy and throughput over Mamba on CIFAR-10. Experiments show that interaction order affects accuracy non-monotonically and symmetry constraints boost learnability. Product Interactions provides a mathematical methodology for analyzing existing architectures and systematically designing new ones.

replace-cross Language Models Struggle to Use Representations Learned In-Context

Authors: Michael A. Lepori, Tal Linzen, Ann Yuan, Katja Filippova

Abstract: Though large language models (LLMs) have enabled great success across a wide variety of tasks, they still appear to fall short of one of the loftier goals of artificial intelligence research: creating an artificial system that can adapt its behavior to radically new contexts upon deployment. One important step towards this goal is to create systems that can induce rich representations of data that are seen in-context, and then flexibly deploy these representations to accomplish goals. Recently, Park et al. (2024) demonstrated that current LLMs are indeed capable of inducing such representation from context (i.e., in-context representation learning). The present study investigates whether LLMs can use these representations to complete simple downstream tasks. We first assess whether open-weights LLMs can use in-context representations for next-token prediction, and then probe models using a novel task, adaptive world modeling. In both tasks, we find evidence that open-weights LLMs struggle to deploy representations of novel semantics that are defined in-context, even if they encode these semantics in their latent representations. Furthermore, we assess closed-source, state-of-the-art reasoning models on the adaptive world modeling task, demonstrating that even the most performant LLMs cannot reliably leverage novel patterns presented in-context. Overall, this work seeks to inspire novel methods for encouraging models to not only encode information presented in-context, but to do so in a manner that supports flexible deployment of this information.

replace-cross ReText: Text Boosts Generalization in Image-Based Person Re-identification

Authors: Timur Mamedov, Karina Kvanchiani, Anton Konushin, Vadim Konushin

Abstract: Generalizable image-based person re-identification (Re-ID) aims to recognize individuals across cameras in unseen domains without retraining. We propose ReText, a novel method trained with textual supervision to enrich visual features with semantic cues. Besides, inspired by recent findings showing that incorporating single-camera data into training improves generalization, we also adopt this strategy. The expanded multi-camera datasets and VLM weights are publicly available at https://github.com/timmzak/ReText.

Abstract: Advances in large language models (LLMs) are rapidly transforming scientific work, yet empirical evidence on how these systems reshape research activities remains limited. Beyond MCQ outcomes, the study documents a labor shift. The researcher's work moved from ``authoring items'' toward {specification, orchestration, verification}, and {governance}. Formalizing constraints, designing rubrics, building validation loops, recovering from tool failures, and auditing provenance constituted the primary activities. We discuss implications for the future of scientific work, including emerging ``AI research operations'' skills required for AI-empowered research pipelines.
